is unique up to asymptotic equivalence. An alternative “dynamic” way of speaking of sharp threshold is to consider the Erdős–Rényi graphs
(G(n,p):p\in[0,1])
as naturally coupled via uniform labelings on the edges as in Section
1.2
; then we write

\tau_{n}:=\inf\{p>0:G(n,p)\in A_{n}\}.

For increasing graph properties, if
p<\tau_{n}
then
G(n,p)\notin A_{n}
whereas if
p>\tau_{n}
then
G(n,p)\in A_{n}
.
Definition
5.1
is then equivalent to the following concentration

\frac{\tau_{n}}{p_{n}}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}1.

Figure 5.1:
Illustration of the sharp threshold transition for an increasing graph property: the functions
x\mapsto\mathbb{P}(G(n,x\cdot p_{n})\in A_{n})
converge pointwise on
[0,\infty)\backslash\{1\}
towards the step function
\mathbf{1}_{x<1}
.

Exercise 5.2
.

Let
A_{n}
be a non-empty increasing graph property. Show that there exists
p_{n}\in(0,1)
so that
A_{n}
has a
weak threshold
at
p_{n}
in the sense that for any sequence
\alpha_{n}\to\infty
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(G(n,p_{n}/\alpha_{n})\in A_{n}\right))\to 0\qquad\mbox{ whereas }\qquad\mathbb{P}(G(n,\alpha_{n}\cdot p_{n})\in A_{n})\to 1\quad\mbox{ as }n\to\infty.

5.1  Connectivity

Probably the most natural question is to ask when the graph
G(n,p)
becomes connected, i.e. to consider the increasing graph property

\mathrm{Connected}_{n}=\{\mathfrak{g}\in\mathbb{G}_{n}:\mathfrak{g}\mbox{ is connected}\}.

As will we see, with high probability this
global
property is in fact ruled by
local
properties, namely the degrees of the vertices in
G(n,p)
. As far as one given vertex
i
is concerned, the situation is quite trivial since for every
i\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
fixed, we have

\mathrm{deg}_{G(n,p)}(i)\overset{(d)}{=}\mathrm{Binomial}(n-1,p),

and so the expected degree of a given vertex is
(n-1)p
. But these degrees are not independent!

5.1.1   Isolated vertices

We shall focus on
isolated
vertices (i.e. of degree
0
), in the Erdős–Rényi random graph. Consider the following increasing graph property

\mathrm{NoIso}_{n}=\{\mathfrak{g}\in\mathbb{G}_{n}:\forall 1\leqslant i\leqslant n,\mathrm{deg}_{\mathfrak{g}}(i)>0\},

and notice that we trivially have
\mathrm{Connected}_{n}\subset\mathrm{NoIso}_{n}
for every
n\geqslant 1
.

Proposition 5.1
.

The sequence
(\mathrm{NoIso}_{n})_{n\geqslant 1}
has a sharp threshold transition for
(G(n,p))_{p\in[0,1]}
at

p_{n}=\frac{\log n}{n}.

Proof.
We use the method of first and second moment. Since the degree of any single vertex in
G(n,p_{n})
follows a
\mathrm{Bin}(n-1,p_{n})
distribution, the first moment method shows that if
X(n,p)
is the number of isolated vertices in
G(n,p)
then

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(G(n,p_{n})\mbox{ has an isolated vertex})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X(n,p_{n})>0)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X(n,p_{n})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(n-1,p_{n})=0)=n(1-p_{n})^{n-1}.

If
p_{n}\geqslant(1+\varepsilon)\frac{\log n}{n}
then the right-hand size clearly tends to
0
as
n\to\infty
and this shows that
G(n,p_{n})
has no isolated vertices w.h.p. in this regime. If now
p_{n}\leqslant(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\log n}{n}
, we deduce from the last display that the expected number of isolated vertices diverges. To guarantee that
\mathbb{P}(G(n,p_{n})\mbox{ has an isolated vertex})\to 1
, we use second moment method (Lemma
1.6
) and compute

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X(n,p_{n})^{2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}\mathbb{P}\left(i\mbox{ and }j\mbox{ are isolated in }G(n,p_{n})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\mathbb{P}(1\mbox{ is isolated})+n(n-1)\mathbb{P}(1\mbox{ and }2\mbox{ are isolated})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n(1-p_{n})^{n-1}+n(n-1)(1-p_{n})^{2n-3}.

Notice that factor in the last display is not exactly equal to
\mathbb{P}(1\mbox{ is isolated})^{2}
since we only count the edge between the vertices
1
and
2

once
. However, in the regime
p_{n}\leqslant(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\log n}{n}
it is easy to see that
\mathbb{E}[X(n,p_{n})]^{2}\sim\mathbb{E}[X(n,p_{n})^{2}]
and so by Lemma
1.6
there are isolated vertices with high probability. ∎

5.1.2   Hitting time theorem

Perhaps surprisingly, as soon as the graph
G(n,p)
has no isolated vertices, it becomes instantaneously connected with high probability:

Theorem 5.2

(Erdős–Rényi (1959))
.

The sequence
(\mathrm{Connected}_{n})_{n\geqslant 1}
has a sharp threshold transition for
(G(n,p))_{p\in[0,1]}
at
p_{n}=\frac{\log n}{n}.
More precisely, in the coupled version of the Erdős–Rényi random graphs, if we set

\tau_{n}=\inf\{p>0:G(n,p)\mbox{ is connected}\}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\theta_{n}=\inf\{p>0:G(n,p)\mbox{ has no isolated vertices}\}

then we have
\mathbb{P}(\tau_{n}=\theta_{n})\to 1
as
n\to\infty
.

We will see another proof of the first part of this result in the next chapter (Proposition
8.6
).

Given Proposition
5.1
, it remains to understand whether the graph
G(n,p)
can have several components which are not made of isolated vertices. We say that a graph
\mathfrak{g}
has the
core property
if it is made of a (usually large, but possibly small) connected component of size at least
2
together with isolated vertices. We denote by
\mathrm{Core}_{n}
the associated set of graphs of
\mathbb{G}_{n}
satisfying the core property. Notice that this property
is not increasing
(nor decreasing) and so some care is needed.

Figure 5.2:
A graph having the core property: a single component (in red in the figure) together with isolated vertices (in blue). This property is however not stable by addition of edges.

Lemma 5.3

(Core property)
.

For any
\frac{2}{3}\frac{\log n}{n}\leqslant p_{n}\leqslant 2\frac{\log n}{n}
we have
G(n,p_{n})\in\mathrm{Core}_{n}
with high probability.

Proof of the lemma.
Actually, the proof will hint to the fact that the (sharp) phase transition for this property appears at
\frac{1}{2}\frac{\log n}{n}
(we put
2/3
to be on safe ground). Let us denote
\mathrm{Cut}\equiv\mathrm{Cut}(n,p)
the number of ways to partition the vertices
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
in two subsets
A\coprod B=\{1,2,\dots,n\}
such that in
G(n,p)
we have

\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}2\leqslant\#A\leqslant\#B,\\
A\mbox{ is connected},\\
\mbox{there is no edge between }A\mbox{ and }B.\end{array}\right.

Notice that if
G(n,p)
does not have the core property, we can find two disjoint clusters of size at least
2
and by adding components we can split the graph into two subsets
A,B
as above (taking for
A
the smallest subset). By the first moment method (applied twice) we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(G(n,p_{n})\mbox{ has no Core})

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Cut}(n,p_{n})\geqslant 1)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Cut}(n,p_{n})]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{n\choose k}\left((1-p_{n})^{n-k}\right)^{k}\mathbb{P}(G(k,p_{n})\mbox{ is connected})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{n\choose k}\left((1-p_{n})^{n-k}\right)^{k}\mathbb{P}(\exists\mbox{ spanning tree in }G(k,p_{n}))

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{n\choose k}\left((1-p_{n})^{n-k}\right)^{k}\mathbb{E}\left[\#\mbox{ spanning trees in }G(k,p_{n})\right]

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{Cor}.\ \ref{cayley}}{=}

\displaystyle\sum_{k=2}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}{n\choose k}\left((1-p_{n})^{n-k}\right)^{k}k^{k-2}(p_{n})^{k-1},

where the factor
(1-p_{n})^{k(n-k)}
counts for the probability that no edge is present between a subset of size
k
and its complement in
G(n,p_{n})
.
Take
\varepsilon>0
small enough so that if
k\leqslant\varepsilon n
we have
\frac{2}{3}k(n-k)\geqslant\frac{7}{12}nk
. For those small
k
we use the bound

\displaystyle{n\choose k}\left((1-p_{n})^{n-k}\right)^{k}k^{k-2}(p_{n})^{k-1}

\displaystyle\underset{\frac{2}{3}\frac{\log n}{n}\leqslant p_{n}\leqslant 2\frac{\log n}{n}}{\leqslant}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cst}^{k}\ \frac{n^{k}}{k!}\exp\left(-\frac{7}{12}kn\frac{\log n}{n}\right)k^{k}\left(\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{k-1}

\displaystyle\underset{k^{k}/k!\leqslant\mathrm{Cst}\cdot\mathrm{e}^{k}}{\leqslant}

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{Cst}\cdot n\cdot n^{-7/12}\cdot\mathrm{e}\cdot\frac{\log n}{n}\right)^{k}\frac{n}{\log n}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{Cst}\cdot n^{-69/120}\right)^{k}n,

where
\mathrm{Cst}
is a universal constant that may vary from line to line. Since
k\geqslant 2
, and
2\times\frac{69}{120}>1
, those bounds are summable in
2\leqslant k\leqslant n/2
and are in fact dominated by the term
k=2
which tends to
0
. For the large
k
, since
n-k\geqslant n/2
and
k\geqslant\varepsilon n
, we have

{n\choose k}\left((1-p_{n})^{n-k}\right)^{k}\leqslant 2^{n}\exp\left(-\varepsilon n\log n/100\right)\leqslant(2n^{-\varepsilon/100})^{n},

and this bound can also by summed over the possible values of
k
to get a vanishing quantity. The lemma is proved. ∎

Proof of Theorem
5.2
.
The combination of Proposition
5.1
with Lemma
5.3
already shows that connectedness has a sharp threshold at
p_{n}=\frac{\log n}{n}
: w.h.p there are still isolated vertices (and so the graph is not connected) at
p=(1-\varepsilon)\frac{\log n}{n}
by Proposition
5.1
whereas there are no isolated vertex at
p_{n}=(1+\varepsilon)\frac{\log n}{n}
and by Lemma
5.3
the graph has the core property at this value of
p_{n}
: it must be connected w.h.p. We cannot directly derive that
\tau_{n}=\theta_{n}
because we cannot apply Lemma
5.3
to the random time
\theta_{n}
. However, the following strengthening of the lemma holds and enables us to conclude that
\tau_{n}=\theta_{n}
w.h.p. :

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(G(n,p)\in\mathrm{Core}_{n}\mbox{\ simultaneously for all }\frac{2}{3}\frac{\log n}{n}\leqslant p\leqslant 2\frac{\log n}{n}\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}1.

(5.1)

To see this, let us start from
p_{n}=\frac{2}{3}\frac{\log n}{n}
where we know that the graph has the core property with high probability by Lemma
5.3
. Denote by
\mathcal{C}_{n}
its core and by
\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{X(n,p_{n})}
its isolated vertices. Recall from the proof of Proposition
5.1
that we have

\mathbb{E}[X(n,p_{n})]=n(1-p_{n})^{{n-1}}\sim n\mathrm{e}^{-\frac{2}{3}\log n}=n^{1/3},

so that by Markov’s inequality the event
{H}_{n}=\{X(n,p_{n})\leqslant n^{5/12}\}
happens with high probability as
n\to\infty
. Conditionally on
G(n,p_{n})
, consider for each isolated
\ell_{i}
the next edge
(\ell_{i}\leftrightarrow x_{i})
adjacent to
\ell_{i}
to be added to
G(n,p_{n})
. These edges are not independent, but conditionally on
G(n,p_{n})
, for each
1\leqslant i\leqslant X(n,p_{n})
, the other extremity
x_{i}
is uniform on
\{1,2,\dots,n\}\backslash\{\ell_{i}\}
. In particular, the probability that
x_{i}
does not belong to the core of
G(n,\frac{2}{3}\frac{\log n}{n})
is

\mathbb{P}(x_{i}\notin\mathcal{C}_{n})=1-\frac{\#\mathcal{C}_{n}}{n}=\frac{X(n,p_{n})-1}{n}\underset{\mathrm{on\ }H_{n}}{\leqslant}n^{-7/12}.

Conditionally on
G(n,p_{n})
and on
H_{n}
, the expected number of
x_{i}\notin\mathcal{C}_{n}
is bounded above by
n^{5/12}\penalty\ \cdot\penalty\ n^{-7/12}\penalty\ =n^{-1/6}
and by the first moment method we deduce that with high probability, for all
1\leqslant i\leqslant X(n,p_{n})
the first edge connected to each isolated vertex
\ell_{i}
after time
p_{n}
will link it to the core
\mathcal{C}_{n}
. In particular, no isolated vertices of
G(n,\frac{2}{3}\frac{\log n}{n})
get connected together and this entails (
5.1
).
∎

We saw above that the variation of the individual degrees in
G(n,p_{n})
rules some large scale geometric properties. This variation disappears when
p_{n}\gg\frac{\log n}{n}
and we leave the following as an exercise for the reader (after having given a look at Lemma
13.8
):

Exercise 5.3

(Range of degrees)
.

For
\beta>0
set
p_{n}=\beta\log n/n
. In
G(n,p_{n})
let
m_{n}
and
M_{n}
respectively be the minimal and maximal vertex degrees. Show that we have

\frac{m_{n}}{\beta\log n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}h(\beta),\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\frac{M_{n}}{\beta\log n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}H(\beta),

where
h(\beta)
and
H(\beta)
are the two solutions to the equation

\beta\cdot I(x)=1,\quad\mbox{ with }I(a):=a\log a-(a-1),

where
h(\beta)=0
for
\beta<1
(there is only one solution). In particular
0=h(1)<H(1)=\mathrm{e}
and
\beta^{-1}(H(\beta)-h(\beta))\to 0
as
\beta\to\infty
.

5.2  Other thresholds via first and second moments

We present a few other sharp thresholds for appearance or disappearance of certain (induced) subgraphs in
G(n,p)
whose proofs are also based on the first and second moment method.

5.2.1   Diameter

In this section, let us focus on the diameter of
G(n,p_{n})
, that is the maximal (graph) distance between any pairs of points in the graph. Of course, by the results of the last section, the diameter is finite only in the regime
p_{n}\geqslant\frac{\log n}{n}(1+o(1))
. In a graph with maximum degree
d\geqslant 3
, by the same argument as in the proof of Proposition
1.3
, the number of vertices at distance less than or equal to
r
from a given origin vertex is at most
1+d+d(d-1)+d(d-1)^{2}+\cdots+d(d-1)^{r-1}=1+\frac{d}{d-2}((d-1)^{r}-1)
. If the graph is connected and has
n
vertices, maximal degree
d
and diameter
r
, we deduce the crude bound

1+\frac{d}{d-2}(d-1)^{r}\geqslant n.

Combining this with the rule of thumb “
\mbox{degrees}\approx\mbox{mean degree}\approx np_{n}
” (valid as soon as
p_{n}\gg\frac{\log n}{n}
by Exercise
5.3
) leads us to postulate that the diameter of
G(n,p_{n})
is roughly
\frac{\log n}{\log np_{n}}
. Let us prove this fact in details for the case
\mathrm{Diameter}=2
which is the smallest non-trivial diameter. We denote by

\mathrm{Diam}^{2}_{n}=\{\mathfrak{g}\in\mathbb{G}_{n}\mbox{ with diameter }\leqslant 2\}

the associated increasing graph property.

Proposition 5.4
.

The sequence
(\mathrm{Diam}^{2}_{n})_{n\geqslant 1}
has a sharp threshold transition for
(G(n,p))_{p\in[0,1]}
at

p_{n}=\sqrt{\frac{2\log n}{n}}.

Proof.
We again use the first and second moment method. The number
D_{n}
of pairs
\{i,j\}
with
i\neq j\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
so that
\mathrm{d}_{G(n,p_{n})}(i,j)>2
is easily computed since this just means that
i
and
j
are not neighbors nor share a neighbor:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[D_{n}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{n\choose 2}\mathbb{P}(1\mbox{ and }2\mbox{ are not neighbors and do not share a neighbor})

(5.2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{n\choose 2}(1-p_{n})\cdot\mathbb{P}(\mbox{3 is not connected to both 1 and 2})^{n-2}

(5.3)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{n\choose 2}(1-p_{n})(1-p_{n}^{2})^{n-2}\sim\frac{n^{2}}{2}\exp(-np_{n}^{2}),

(5.4)

when
p_{n}\to 0
. Hence if
p_{n}\geqslant(1+\varepsilon)\sqrt{2\log n/n}
the expected number of vertices at distance strictly larger than
2
vanishes as
n\to\infty
. By the first moment method, this implies that w.h.p. the diameter of
G(n,p_{n})
is less than or equal to
2
in this regime (it is then equal to
2
unless
p_{n}=1-o(n^{-2})
; the diameter being
1
if and only if all edges are present).

We now suppose that
p_{n}=(1-\varepsilon)\sqrt{2\log n/n}
so that
n^{2}\mathrm{e}^{-np_{n}^{2}}\to\infty
and the expectation of
D_{n}
diverges. To prove that w.h.p. there are vertices which do not share a neighbor we compute the second moment of
D_{n}
:

\mathbb{E}[D_{n}^{2}]=\sum_{i,j,k,l}\mathbb{P}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}i\mbox{ and }j\mbox{ have no common neighbor}\\
i\mbox{ and }j\mbox{ are not neighbors}\\
k\mbox{ and }l\mbox{ have no common neighbor}\\
k\mbox{ and }l\mbox{ are not neighbors}\end{array}\right).

In the case when
i,j,k,l
are all distinct, the possibilities of the induced subgraph
G(n,p_{n})[\{i,j,k,l\}]
on
i,j,k,l
are displayed in the following figure which account for a probability
(1-p_{n})^{6}+4p_{n}(1-p_{n})^{5}+2p_{n}^{2}(1-p_{n})^{4}
which tends to
1
as
n\to\infty
, and then the status of all other
n-4
vertices contribute to a probability equal to
(1-p_{n}^{2})^{2(n-4)}
.

Figure 5.3:
The possibilities for the induced subgraph on two pairs of vertices (here in red and blue) so that the distance between elements of each pair is at least
3
.

The contribution of this case to the sum is then asymptotic to

\frac{n^{4}}{4}\mathrm{e}^{-2np_{n}^{2}}\sim\mathbb{E}[D_{n}]^{2}.

Similarly, the contribution of overlapping pairs, i.e.  the case when there are only three (resp. two) vertices among
i,j,k,l
is negligible in front of the last term (we leave the details to the fearless reader). Since
\mathbb{E}[D_{n}]
diverges, only the first case prevails and
\mathbb{E}[D_{n}^{2}]\sim\mathbb{E}[D_{n}]^{2}
. By the second moment method (Lemma
1.6
) we conclude that
\mathbb{P}(D_{n}>0)\to 1
as
n\to\infty
. ∎

5.2.2   Ramsey theory, max-clique,
\mathrm{P=NP}
and alien invasion

A consequence of Ramsey’s
1
1

1

Frank Ramsey (1903–1930), English
theorem (for two colors) is that for any
k\geqslant 1
, there exists a number
\mathcal{R}(k)
such that every (simple) graph with more than
\mathcal{R}(k)
vertices contains either a
clique
(an induced subgraph equal to the complete graph) of size
k
or an
independent set
(induced subgraph with only isolated vertices) with size
k
. Perhaps surprisingly, even the value of the Ramsey number
\mathcal{R}(5)
is unknown although it must lies in
\{43,44,45,\dots,48\}
(see wikipedia). The bounds on
\mathcal{R}(k)
are exponential in
k
and actually random Erdős–Rényi graphs achieve almost the best possible:

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
K_{n}
and
I_{n}
respectively be the maximal size of a clique and of an independent set in
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}})
. Then we have

\frac{K_{n}}{\log_{2}n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}2,\qquad\frac{I_{n}}{\log_{2}n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}2.

Proof.
Notice that
I_{n}=K_{n}
in distribution since
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}})
is self-dual in the sense that if we switch the status of all edges we obtain the same (law of) random graph. As the reader may have foreseen, we first compute the expected value of
\chi_{k}
, the number of induced
k
-cliques in our random graph:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\chi_{k}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\#\left\{k-\mathrm{cliques\ }\mbox{ in }G(n,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}})\right\}\right]={n\choose k}2^{-{k\choose 2}}.

(5.5)

It is easy to see that this tends to
0
if
k\equiv k_{n}>(2+\varepsilon)\log_{2}n
as
n\to\infty
. So by the first moment method, the size of the largest clique is less than
(2+\varepsilon)\log_{2}n
w.h.p.

We now compute the second moment of the number of
k
-cliques and obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\left(\chi_{k}\right)^{2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S,S^{\prime}\subset\{1,\dots,n\}\\
\#S=\#S^{\prime}=k\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}(\mbox{the induced graphs on }S,S^{\prime}\mbox{ are cliques})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{n\choose k}2^{-{k\choose 2}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{k}{k\choose\ell}{n-k\choose k-\ell}\cdot 2^{-{k\choose 2}+{\ell\choose 2}}.

To get the second line, we pick the first clique
S\subset\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and then partition according to the intersection
\ell=\#(S\cap S^{\prime})
. If
S^{\prime}
shares some vertices with
S
, it is easier for it to be a clique since we only need to open
{k\choose 2}-{\ell\choose 2}
edges because the
{\ell\choose 2}
edges in-between common vertices of
S
and
S^{\prime}
are already present in (the induced subgraph of)
S
. Notice that when
\ell=0
or
\ell=1
the edges in-between vertices of
S
and
S^{\prime}
are pairwise distinct. We then leave to the reader the tedious task of checking that the above sum is dominated by the term corresponding to
\ell=0
when
k=k_{n}\leqslant(2-\varepsilon)\log_{2}n
, i.e. that
\mathbb{E}[\chi_{k_{n}}^{2}]\sim\mathbb{E}[\chi_{k_{n}}]^{2}
. By the second moment method (Lemma
1.6
), we deduce that indeed w.h.p, there are
k
-cliques for
k\leqslant(2-\varepsilon)\log_{2}n
. ∎

Remark 5.1

(Ramsey and Erdős)
.

Recall the definition of
\mathcal{R}(k)
as the smallest integer so that a graph with size larger than
\mathcal{R}(k)
must contain a clique or an independent set of size
k
. Proving that
\mathcal{R}(k)<\infty
is not trivial and is in fact Ramsey’s theorem. However, from (
5.5
) we deduce that if
{n\choose k}2^{-{k\choose 2}}<1/2
then the expectation of
\mathcal{X}_{k}+\mathcal{I}_{k}
is less than
1
where
\mathcal{I}_{k}
is the number of independent sets of size
k
, and this implies that
\mathcal{X}_{k}+\mathcal{I}_{k}
is not almost surely larger than
1
or equivalently that there
exists
a graph on
n
vertices which has no clique nor independent set of size
k
. In other words,

\mathcal{R}(k)>n.

Although this reasoning (one of the first instances of the
probabilistic method
) might appear simplistic, finding such a graph is a very difficult problem. Quoting Spencer
[
107
]
:

“For the Ramsey function
\mathcal{R}(k)
no construction is known that gives nearly the lower bound that can be derived from the [above] proof… Erdős asks us to imagine an alien force, vastly more powerful than us, landing on Earth and demanding the value of
\mathcal{R}(5)
or they will destroy our planet. In that case, he claims, we should marshall all our computers and all our mathematicians and attempt to find the value. But suppose, instead, that they ask for
\mathcal{R}(6)
. In that case, he believes, we should attempt to destroy the aliens.”

Remark 5.2

(Very sharp threshold)
.

A careful inspection of the proof (and precise estimations) enable to reinforce Proposition
5.5
as follows: There exists an integer
k_{n}\sim 2\log_{2}n
so that we have

\mathbb{P}(K_{n}\in\{k_{n},k_{n}+1\})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}1,

in other words, the maximal size of a clique is concentrated on only two values!

Remark 5.3

(Finding cliques)
.

Although the previous result entails the existence of cliques of size
\approx 2\log_{2}n
in
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}})
, finding them is a very difficult task. Indeed, given a graph of size
n
, say by its adjacency matrix, an exhaustive search of a clique of size
\log_{2}n
costs

{n\choose\log_{2}n}\approx n^{\log_{2}n}\mbox{ which is superpolynomial in }n.

It is known that finding the max clique in a (deterministic) graph is a NP-complete task, but more surprisingly it is open as of today whether we can find a clique of size
(1+\varepsilon)\log_{2}n
in
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}})
– hence a bit above half of the maximal size– in a polynomial time! See the exercise below to find a clique of size approximatively
\log_{2}n
.

Exercise 5.4

(Greedy construction of a clique)
.

In
G(n,1/2)
, whose vertex set is
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
, consider the following construction of a clique: Start with the vertex
X_{0}=1
. By induction, if
X_{0}<X_{1}<\dots<X_{k}
have been constructed so that all edges
X_{i}\leftrightarrow X_{j}
are present in
G(n,1/2)
for
0\leqslant i<j\leqslant k
, let
X_{k+1}
be the smallest vertex larger than
X_{k}
which is connected to all
X_{0},\dots,X_{k}
in
G(n,1/2)
. If there is no such vertex the construction stops and output a complete induced subgraph, i.e. a clique with
K_{n}
vertices. Let
G_{1},G_{2},\dots
be independent geometric variable with success parameter
2^{-i}
i.e.

\mathbb{P}(G_{i}=\ell)=2^{-i}(1-2^{-i})^{\ell-1}.

1.

Show that
K_{n}=\min\{k\geqslant 1:1+\sum_{i=1}^{k}G_{i}>n\}
in law.

2.

Deduce that

\frac{K_{n}}{\log_{2}n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}1.

5.3  Higher moments

So far, we have established sharp thresholds for graph properties in
G(n,p)
only using the first and second moment. When there is no sharp threshold or for more refined probabilistic estimates such as convergence in distribution, we need to control higher moments. We shall exhibit two examples when we need to do so: the
Poisson paradigm
and the convergence of the spectral measure. Let us recall the classic method of moments:

Lemma 5.6

(Method of moments)
.

Let
(\mu_{n}:n\geqslant 0)
be probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
(resp. random real variables
X_{n}
) such that for any
k\geqslant 0
, there exists
C_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
such that we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mu_{n}(\mathrm{d}x)\cdot x^{k}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}C_{k},\qquad\left(\mbox{resp.}\quad\mathbb{E}[X_{n}^{k}]\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}C_{k}\right),

in particular the above moments all exist. We suppose furthermore that for some
M>0
we have
|C_{k}|\leqslant M^{k}k!
for all
k\geqslant 0
. Then there exists a probability measure
\mu
on
\mathbb{R}
(resp. a random variable
X
) such that
\mu_{n}\to\mu
in distribution as
n\to\infty
(resp.
X_{n}\to X
in law).

Proof of the lemma:
Since
\mu_{n}
have bounded first moment,
(\mu_{n})_{n\geqslant 0}
is tight and by dominated convergence its potential limits
\mu
have the same moments
C_{k}
for
k\geqslant 0
. However the growth condition
|C_{k}|\leqslant M^{k}k!
implies that the moment generating function of (any possible limit)
\mu
has a positive radius of convergence. By Fubini, the Laplace transform
\mathcal{L}_{\mu}
of
\mu
also has a positive radius of convergence and, as every analytic function,
\mathcal{L}_{\mu}
is determined by its derivatives at
0
: it follows that
\mu
is determined by its moments. In particular
\mu
is unique, and
\mu_{n}\to\mu
weakly as
n\to\infty
. The translation in terms of random variables is straightforward.∎

Remark 5.4

(A trivial case:
\sigma_{n}^{2}\to 0
)
.

When we have
\mathbb{E}[X_{n}^{1}]\to C_{1}
and
\mathbb{E}[X_{n}^{2}]\to C_{1}^{2}
as
n\to\infty
then we automatically have
X_{n}\to C_{1}
in distribution (and in probability). This was the case in most of the results of the previous section.

5.3.1   The Poisson paradigm

In this section, we explain informally why the property

\mathrm{Cycle}_{n}=\{\mathfrak{g}\in\mathbb{G}_{n}:\mathfrak{g}\mbox{ contains a simple cycle as subgraph}\},

actually has
no
sharp threshold transition for
G(n,p)
. To fix ideas, let us look at the smallest non trivial cycle and let
\Delta(n,p)
be the number of induced triangles in
G(n,p)
. One straightforwardly computes:

\mathbb{E}[\Delta(n,p)]={n\choose 3}p^{3},

and so when
p=p_{n}=o(1/n)
, by the first moment method, there is no triangle inside
G(n,p)
with high probability. When
p\gg 1/n
then the expectation of the number of triangles blows up and we can show that the variance of the number of triangles is comparable to its squared mean (exercise!) so that by the second moment method, there is a triangle inside
G(n,p)
with high probability. However when
p=\frac{c}{n}
, the last expectation converge towards
c^{3}/6
and actually
\Delta(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
converges towards a Poisson variable of parameter
c^{3}/6
, this is the
Poisson paradigm
: the sum of many indicators of small probability which are roughly independent give a Poisson random variable in the limit. One way to prove it is to show that all moments of
\Delta(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
converge towards the moments of
\mathfrak{P}(c^{3}/6)
and use the method of moments (Lemma
5.6
). This requires a careful but not unbearable analysis which we will not do in this course.

In particular, existence of a triangle in
G(n,p)
does not have a sharp threshold transition: its probability goes from
0
to
1
when
p
ranges in the scale
1/n
but does not jump from
0
to
1
abruptly “in one scale”. Actually, the Poisson paradigm can be extended to consider cycles of length
3,4,5\dots
simultaneously and actually they behave as independent Poisson variables with parameters
c^{k}/(2k)
as
n\to\infty
(each equivalence class of
k
ordered points representing the same cycle has
2k
members). In particular, for
c\in(0,1)
we have

\mathbb{P}(G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})\mbox{ has no simple cycle})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\exp\left(-\sum_{k\geqslant 3}\frac{c^{k}}{2k}\right)=\sqrt{(1-c)}\mathrm{e}^{\frac{1}{4}c(c+2)}.

Figure 5.4:
A plot of
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})\mbox{ has no simple cycle})
for
c\in[0,1]
. In particular the appearance of a simple cycle has no sharp threshold, but such a cycle should appear before
c=1
.

Recalling the first section of this chapter, although the presence of isolated vertices obeys a sharp threshold, if we refine the scale, the Poisson paradigm also appears and it is known that the number of isolated vertices in
G\left(n,\frac{\log n+c}{n}\right)
for
c\in\mathbb{R}
converges in distribution towards a Poisson variable of parameter
\mathrm{e}^{-c}
. In particular, we have the “double exponential limit of Erdős–Rényi”

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(G\left(n,\frac{\log n}{n}+\frac{c}{n}\right)\mbox{ has no isolated vertex}\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-c}},

(5.6)

see Theorem
8.6
in Chapter
8
for an proof of it.

Exercise 5.5

(An application to random matrix)
.

Consider i.i.d. vectors
X_{1},\dots,X_{k},\dots\in\{0,1\}^{n}
such that
X_{i}
has only zeros except at two positions chosen uniformly at random among the
{n\choose 2}
possibilities. Evaluate
\mathbb{P}(X_{1},\dots,X_{k}\mbox{ are linearly independent over }\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
as a function of
k
(for
n
fixed but large).

5.3.2   Spectrum

In this section we shall study
G(n,p)
from a spectral point of view. As the reader will see, this boils down to computing the (expected) number of (possibly backtracking) cycles in
G(n,p)
. In this section we focus on the case when

\displaystyle p=\frac{c}{n},\quad
with
c>0
fixed.

Let
A=A^{(n)}_{c}
the adjacency matrix of
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
. More precisely, it is a symmetric square
n\times n
matrix where
A_{i,j}=1
if
i
and
j
are neighbors in
G(n,p)
. Notice that the entries of
A
are not centered and by convention we put
A_{i,i}=0
. As any symmetric real matrix,
A^{(n)}_{c}
has a spectral decomposition and
n
eigenvalues

\lambda_{1}^{(n)}\leqslant\lambda_{2}^{(n)}\leqslant\cdots\leqslant\lambda_{n}^{(n)}.

We shall be interested in the empirical spectral measure

\Lambda^{(n)}_{c}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\delta_{\lambda_{i}^{(n)}},

which is then a random probability measure (hence its law is an element of
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathbb{R}))
where
\mathcal{M}_{1}(\mathcal{X})
is the set of all probability measures on
\mathcal{X}
). The following theorem shows that this measure converges towards a deterministic measure:

Theorem 5.7

(Convergence of the spectral measure)
.

With the above notation, for
c>0
we have the following convergence in probability

\Lambda^{(n)}_{c}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}\mathcal{L}_{c},

where
\mathcal{L}_{c}
is a (deterministic) probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
.

The above convergence in probability just means that for any function
f\in\mathcal{C}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
with compact support we have

\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda^{(n)}_{c}(\mathrm{d}x)f(x)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{L}_{c}(\mathrm{d}x)f(x).

It is possible to properly speak of convergence (in probability or in distribution) for random measures by defining a topology on probability distributions on
\mathbb{R}
. We refer to the authoritative reference
[
71
]
for details about convergence of random measures. The limiting (deterministic) measure
\mathcal{L}_{c}
is poorly understood as of today (e.g. the decomposition of
\mathcal{L}_{c}
in atomic and continuous part…).

Figure 5.5:
Simulations of
\Lambda^{(n)}_{c}
for
c=2
and
c=10
.

Partial proof.
We shall only prove a weak version of the theorem, namely that the
expected
empirical measure converges. To prove this, we shall use the method of moments (Lemma
5.6
) and prove convergence of the moments i.e.

\mathbb{E}\left[\int\Lambda^{(n)}_{c}(\mathrm{d}x)\cdot x^{k}\right]=\int\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[\Lambda^{(n)}_{c}]}_{(*)}(\mathrm{d}x)\cdot x^{k}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathcal{L}_{c}(\mathrm{d}x)\cdot x^{k}\right]=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\left[\mathcal{L}_{c}\right]}_{(**)}(\mathrm{d}x)\cdot x^{k},

where
(*)
and
(**)
are the expected measures which are deterministic probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
. The convergence in probability of the random measure
\Lambda^{(n)}_{c}
is obtained by further establishing concentration of the empirical moments (e.g. by computing second moments), which we shall skip in these notes, see
[
120
,
73
]
for details.

Even the problem of the convergence of expectation of moments of
\mathbb{E}[\Lambda^{(n)}_{c}]
might be complicated since the construction of the eigenvalues of
A^{(n)}_{c}
is very intricate. The idea is to use the spectral decomposition and to take the expected trace of the powers of the matrix
A^{(n)}_{c}
: indeed by invariance of the trace under change of basis we have

n\cdot\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Lambda^{(n)}_{c}(\mathrm{d}x)\cdot x^{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\lambda_{i}^{(n)})^{k}=\mathrm{Tr}\left(\left(A^{(n)}_{c}\right)^{k}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{j=i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}A_{i_{1},i_{2}}A_{i_{2},i_{3}}\dots A_{i_{k},i_{1}}.

After taking the expectation, all we need is to isolate the contribution of order
n
in the above sum. We will gather the terms in
\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\mathbb{E}[A_{i_{1},i_{2}}A_{i_{2},i_{3}}\dots A_{i_{k},i_{1}}]
which share the same the combinatorial structure for the cycle
i_{1}\to i_{2}\to\cdots\to i_{k}\to i_{1}
and represent it by a diagram. More precisely, we shall partition this sum according to the underlying multigraph
\mathfrak{g}
obtained by identifying in the “free" cycle
i_{1}\to i_{2}\to\cdots\to i_{k}\to i_{1}
the indices
i_{j}
corresponding to the same vertex
\in\{1,\dots,n\}
. Those graphs are usually called Feynman’s
2
2

2

Richard Feynman (1918–1988), American
diagrams in the physics literature. See Figure
5.6
for examples.

Figure 5.6:
Expanding the expectation using a sum over Feynman diagrams. The only non zero asymptotic contribution comes from the trees with possibly several edges.

Once a multi-graph
\mathfrak{g}
with a rooted oriented spanning path is fixed, if
v
is its number of vertices and
e
its number of edges after collapsing the possible multi-edges, the corresponding contribution in the above sum is equal to

{n\choose v}\left(\frac{c}{n}\right)^{e}\underset{n\to\infty}{\sim}\frac{c^{e}}{v!}n^{v-e}.

Hence, the main contribution to the above expectation is provided by Feynman diagrams for which
v-e
is maximal: those are finite trees (for which we have
v-e=1
). More precisely, in this case
k=2\ell
must be even and those objects are finite (non plane) trees with
e\leqslant\ell
edges together with an image of the rooted
k
-cycle
1\to 2\to\dots\to 2\ell\to 1
which is surjective. If we denote by
\mathrm{Fey}(2\ell,e)
the number of such combinatorial objects then we can summarize the discussion by

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\left[\int\Lambda^{(n)}_{c}(\mathrm{d}x)\cdot x^{k}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sum_{j=i_{1},\dots,i_{k}}\mathbb{E}[A_{i_{1},i_{2}}A_{i_{2},i_{3}}\dots A_{i_{k},i_{1}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{k=2\ell\mathrm{\ is\ even}}\sum_{e=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{Fey}(2\ell,e)\frac{c^{e}}{(e+1)!}:=C_{k}.

To check the growth condition on
C_{k}
needed in Lemma
5.6
, notice that

\sum_{e=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{Fey}(2\ell,e)\frac{c^{e}}{(e+1)!}\leqslant c^{\ell}\sum_{e=1}^{\ell}\mathrm{Fey}(2\ell,e)\leqslant c^{\ell}\underbrace{\#\{\mbox{partitions of }\{1,2,\dots,2\ell\}\}}_{\mbox{ Bell number}}\leqslant c^{\ell}(2\ell)^{2\ell}\leqslant M^{k}k!,

for some
M>0
. However, the number of diagrams corresponding to the moment of order
k
grows quicker than exponentially: consider the case when the underlying tree is a star with
\sqrt{n}
vertices decorated by a walk of length
n
, it is easy to see that there are at least
(\sqrt{n})^{n-\sqrt{n}}
diagrams and this in particular implies that the limiting measure
\mathcal{L}_{c}
has unbounded support. ∎

A warning to conclude: Thresholds and expectation thresholds.

Before closing this chapter, let us warn the reader that the first (and second) moment method, although powerful, does not always yield the correct thresholds for typical appearance of induced subgraph. Consider the following example of the “pan graph”

By the first moment, the mean number of such induced graphs in
G(n,p)
is
{n\choose 7}p^{9}
and this blows up when
p\gg n^{-7/9}
. The naïve guess is then
p_{n}\approx n^{-7/9}
for the (weak) threshold of appearance of the pan graph in
G(n,p_{n})
. But if we focus on the appearance of the square with diagonals, we would guess a threshold of order
p_{n}\approx n^{-2/3}
. How come could the second threshold be larger than the first one since we consider a smaller subgraph? The reason is that the first guess
p_{n}\approx n^{-7/9}
based on the first moment method is incorrect: with small probability, a square with diagonals may appear in
G(n,n^{-7/9+\varepsilon})
but then has many different “tails” causing a blow up of the expectation of such induced subgraphs… The expectation threshold conjecture of Kahn & Kalai
[
70
]
states that up to a multiplicative factor of
\log n
the location of the (weak) threshold (see Exercise
5.2
) is given by the above first moment method applied to all subgraphs and taking the maximum. This far-reaching conjecture was recently proved
[
96
]
.

Bibliographical notes.
The Erdős–Rényi model is probably the simplest and the most studied random graph model. It is a wonderful playground for combinatorics and probability. It has many variations and descendants such as the stochastic block model, the rank
1
model, the configuration model… which are more realistic models for real-life networks. The literature on this topic is vast, see e.g. the recent monograph
[
113
]
or the classic books
[
24
,
69
]
. There are also lecture notes available on the web such as
[
26
,
23
]
and
[
119
]
for applications in statistics. Reading the original papers
[
52
,
53
,
54
]
of Erdős & Rényi is still very inspiring. Theorem
5.2
is proved in
[
52
]
. See the nice note
[
35
]
for an application of random graph theory to sparse random matrices (as in Exercise
5.5
).

Hints for exercises.

Exercise
5.1
: By Cayley’s formula the probability is equal to
n^{n-2}p^{n-1}(1-p)^{{n\choose 2}-(n-1)}
and is maximal at
p=\frac{2}{n}
.

Exercise
5.2
: Put
p_{n}
such that
\mathbb{P}(G(n,p_{n})\in A_{n})=\frac{1}{2}
. See Bollobas & Thomason
[
25
]
.

Exercise
5.3
: Use first and second moment method.

Exercise
5.5
: Consider the graph whose vertices are the vectors and where there is an edge between two vectors if they share a non-zero coordinate in common. Then there is a non-trivial
\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}
relation iff the graph contains a cycle.

VI
:
Birth of a giant
1
, via
\varepsilon
-cut

Comment Gargantua nasquit en façon bien estrange. (Rabelais)

Figure 6.1:
A large
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
graph with
n=1000
and
c
equals (from left to right) to

0.1\quad 0.5\quad 1\quad 1.1\quad\frac{\log n}{2}\quad 2\log n.

We see the emergence of a giant component around
c\approx 1
and that the graph becomes connected around
c\approx\log n
(see Theorem
5.2
).

We continue the study of the geometry of
G(n,p)
as
p
increases and prove a phase transition for the size of the clusters in
G(n,p)
: suddenly a “giant component” carrying a positive proportion of the vertices appears around
p_{n}=\frac{1}{n}
. More precisely, for a (finite) graph
\mathfrak{g}
and a vertex
v\in\mathrm{V}(\mathfrak{g})
we denote by
\mathcal{C}^{v}(\mathfrak{g})
the connected component of
v
inside
\mathfrak{g}
. We also denote by
\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}(\mathfrak{g}),\mathrm{C}^{{\max}}_{2}(\mathfrak{g}),\dots
the sizes (number of vertices) of the connected components of
\mathfrak{g}
in non-increasing order. We write
\mathcal{C}_{\max}
for a connected component of maximal size (with ties broken using the labelings of the vertices). Sometimes we drop the notation
(\mathfrak{g})
when the underlying graph is clear from the context. The main theorem of this part is the following:

Theorem 6.1
.

Birth of a giantThere is a sharp threshold transition for the existence of a giant connected component at
p_{n}=\frac{1}{n}
. More precisely, if
p_{n}=\frac{c}{n}
then inside
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
:

•

Subcritical: If
\mathbf{c<1}
then there exists
A>0
depending on
c>0
such that w.h.p. we have
\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}(G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}}))\leqslant A\log n
.

•

Supercritical: If
\mathbf{c>1}
then there exists
A>0
depending on
c>0
such that w.h.p. we have
\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{2}(G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}}))\penalty\ \leqslant\penalty\ A\log n
whereas
n^{-1}\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}(G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}}))\to(1-\alpha(c))
in probability where
\alpha(c)
is the smallest solution in
(0,1]
to the equation

\displaystyle\alpha(c)=\mathrm{e}^{-c(1-\alpha(c))}.

(6.1)

•

Critical: If
\mathbf{c=1}
then the vector
(n^{-2/3}\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{i}(G(n,{\textstyle\frac{1}{n}})))_{i\geqslant 1}
converges in law in the finite dimensional sense towards a positive infinite vector in
\ell^{2}
. This vector is in fact the ordered version of the length of the excursions of the function
t\mapsto B_{t}-\frac{t^{2}}{2}
above its running infimum (sic!).

The goal of the following three chapters is to prove the above result (multiple times). We will actually only prove points
(i)
and
(ii)
and just provide an upper bound for the size of the largest component for point
(iii)
(see also Proposition
8.5
in a slightly different model). The intuition behind Theorem
6.1
is that the local neighborhood around a given vertex in
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
looks like a BGW tree with offspring distribution
\mathrm{Poisson}(c)
, see Proposition
6.3
. When
c<1
such a random tree dies out almost surely (in fact very quickly) and all the connected components in
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
are small. On the contrary, if
c>1
then the BGW process survives with positive probability equal to
1-\alpha(c)
(see Proposition
3.10
and Theorem
4.3
): in the finite setting this means that the component of the vertex in question is very large (a giant component). It turns out that this giant component is unique so that its density is asymptotically
1-\alpha(c)
, and the remaining components are small. We prove a weaker version of the above theorem using this sketch in this chapter then turn to a more modern proof using an exploration technique and estimates on random skip-free walks similar to those used in Chapter
4
. This proof is shortened in Chapter
8
by tricking a little the graph.

In this chapter we give a “first moment” proof of a weak version of Theorem
6.1
which is close in spirit to the historical proof of Erdős & Rényi
[
53
]
. We first study the law of the connected components in
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
and show that they are indeed described by BGW trees with Poisson
(c)
increments. We then use a first moment argument on
\varepsilon
-cuts together with a “sprinkling” idea to deduce the existence of a giant component in the supercritical regime:

Theorem 6.2

(The giant, weak version)
.

For
c>0
denote by
\alpha(c)
the smallest solution in
(0,1]
to the equation
\alpha(c)=\mathrm{e}^{-c(1-\alpha(c))}
in particular
\alpha(c)=1
when
c\leqslant 1
. Then we have

\frac{\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}}{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}1-\alpha(c)\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\frac{\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{2}}{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}0.

6.1  The local limit

We denote by
\mathcal{C}(n,p)\equiv\mathcal{C}^{1}(G(n,p))
the cluster of the vertex
1
in
G(n,p)
which we see as a random labeled graph where its vertices have been relabeled in increasing order by
1,2,\dots,\#\mathcal{C}(n,p)
. We write
\mathrm{T}(c)
for a Bienaymé–Galton–Watson plane tree with offspring distribution Poisson
(c)
and denote by
\mathcal{T}(c)
the Cayley tree obtained by labeling its root by
1
and the rest of its vertices by
2,3,\dots,\#\mathrm{T}(c)
uniformly at random. We put
\mathcal{T}(c)=\dagger
(a cemetery point) if
\mathrm{T}(c)
is infinite.

Proposition 6.3
.

Fix
c>0
and suppose that
p_{n}\sim\frac{c}{n}
as
n\to\infty
. For
k\geqslant 1
and for any connected labeled graph
\mathfrak{g}\in\mathbb{G}_{k}
we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(n,p_{n})=\mathfrak{g})=\mathrm{e}^{-k\cdot c}\frac{c^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}\mathbf{1}_{\displaystyle\mathfrak{g}\mbox{ is a Cayley tree}}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{T}(c)=\mathfrak{g}).

Proof.
If the connected labeled graph
\mathfrak{g}
with
k
vertices and
\ell
edges is fixed, we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})=\mathfrak{g})\quad=\quad{n-1\choose k-1}(1-p_{n})^{k(n-k)+{k\choose 2}-\ell}p_{n}^{\ell}

Taking limits as
n\to\infty
, the above display tends to
0
if
\ell\geqslant k
and towards
\mathrm{e}^{-k\cdot c}\frac{c^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}
if
\ell=k-1
. In the latter case, this formula coincides with the probability that
\mathcal{T}(c)
lands on the tree
\mathfrak{g}
as seen in the proof of Proposition
4.9
. ∎

In particular, when
c\leqslant 1
, since
\mathrm{T}(c)
is almost surely finite we have that

\sum_{k\geqslant 1}k^{k-2}\mathrm{e}^{-k\cdot c}\frac{c^{k-1}}{(k-1)!}=1

so that
\#\mathcal{C}(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})\to\#\mathrm{T}(c)
in distribution as
n\to\infty
. We recall from Definition
3.1
that the latter is distributed according to the Borel–Tanner distribution with parameter
c
. As a quick corollary we can deduce that there is no giant component when
c\leqslant 1
: If
\mathcal{C}_{\max}
in a largest connected component in
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
we have

\displaystyle 0\xleftarrow[n\to\infty]{}\mathbb{P}(\#\mathcal{C}(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})\geqslant\varepsilon n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}^{i}(G(n,\frac{c}{n}))\varepsilon n)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}\geqslant\varepsilon n}\mathbf{1}_{i\in\mathcal{C}^{\max}_{1}}\right]\geqslant\frac{\varepsilon n}{n}\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}\geqslant\varepsilon n).

More precisely, we will see below that the proportion of vertices belonging to “big clusters” is concentrated. But before that let us state an easy lemma whose proof is straightfoward:

Lemma 6.4
.

Conditionally on
\mathcal{C}^{1}(G(n,p))
the remaining graph
1
1

1

with vertices relabeled in increasing order

G(n,p)\backslash\mathcal{C}^{1}(G(n,p))
has law
G(n-\#\mathcal{C}^{1}(G(n,p)))
.

Corollary 6.5
.

With
\alpha(c)
as defined in Theorem
6.2
, for all
A\in\{0,1,2,\dots\}
we have

n^{-1}\,\#\left\{1\leqslant i\leqslant n:\#\mathcal{C}^{i}\big({\textstyle G(n,\frac{c}{n})}\big)\leqslant A\right\}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}\mathbb{P}(\#\mathrm{T}(c)\leqslant A)\xrightarrow[A\to\infty]{}\alpha(c).

Proof.
If
N_{n}(A)=\#\left\{1\leqslant i\leqslant n:\#\mathcal{C}^{i}\big({\textstyle G(n,\frac{c}{n})}\big)\leqslant A\right\}
, by the previous proposition, we have the asymptotic of the expectation:

\mathbb{E}[N_{n}(A)]=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}(\#\mathcal{C}^{i}\leqslant A)=n\mathbb{P}(\#\mathcal{C}(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})\leqslant A)\underset{n\to\infty}{\sim}n\cdot\mathbb{P}(\#\mathrm{T}(c)\leqslant A),

and this asymptotic actually holds as soon as
p_{n}\sim\frac{c}{n}
. Using
\mathcal{C}^{i}=\mathcal{C}^{i}(G(n,c/n))
as a shorthand notation, the second moment is easily bounded:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[N_{n}(A)^{2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}\mathbb{P}(\#\mathcal{C}^{i}\leqslant A\mathrm{\ and\ }\#\mathcal{C}^{j}\leqslant A)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{1\leqslant i,j\leqslant n}\left(\mathbb{P}(\#\mathcal{C}^{i}\leqslant A\mbox{ and }j\in\mathcal{C}^{i})+\mathbb{P}(\#\mathcal{C}^{i}\leqslant A\mbox{ and }\#\mathcal{C}^{j}\leqslant A\mbox{ and }j\notin\mathcal{C}^{i})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle n\mathbb{E}\left[\#\mathcal{C}^{1}\mathbf{1}_{\#\mathcal{C}^{1}\leqslant A}\right]+n(n-1)\mathbb{P}\left(\#\mathcal{C}^{1}\leqslant A\mbox{ and }\#\mathcal{C}^{2}\leqslant A\mbox{ and }2\notin\mathcal{C}^{1}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle nA+n(n-1)\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbf{1}_{\#\mathcal{C}^{1}\leqslant A}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\#\mathcal{C}^{2}\leqslant A\mbox{ and }2\notin\mathcal{C}^{1}}\mid\mathcal{C}^{1}\}\right]\Big]

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{Lemma\ \ref{lem:condcluster}}}{=}

\displaystyle nA+n(n-1)\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\#\mathcal{C}^{1}\leqslant A}\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbf{1}_{2\notin\mathcal{C}_{1}}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbf{1}_{\#\mathcal{C}^{2}\leqslant A}\big|\{2\notin\mathcal{C}^{1}\}\mbox{ and }\mathcal{C}^{1}\big]}_{\mathbb{P}(\#\mathcal{C}(N,\frac{c}{n})\leqslant A)}\Big|\mathcal{C}^{1}\Big]\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle nA+n(n-1)\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{N}{n-1}\cdot\mathbb{P}\left(\#\mathcal{C}\left(N,\frac{c}{n}\right)\leqslant A\right)\right],

where we used Lemma
6.4
to argue that once conditioned on the cluster
\mathcal{C}^{1}
, the remaining graph is distributed as
G(N,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
where
N=n-\#\mathcal{C}^{1}
, so that by symmetry
\mathbb{P}(2\notin\mathcal{C}^{1}\mid\mathcal{C}_{1})=\frac{N}{n-1}
and
\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\#\mathcal{C}^{2}\leqslant A}\mid\{2\notin\mathcal{C}^{1}\}\mbox{ and }\mathcal{C}^{1}\right]=\mathbb{P}(\#\mathcal{C}(N,c/n)\leqslant A)
. Since
N\sim n
, we can apply the lemma above once more and deduce that the previous display is asymptotic to
n^{2}\mathbb{P}(\#\mathrm{T}(c)\leqslant A)^{2}
. We deduce that
\mathbb{E}[n^{-1}N_{n}(A)]\to\mathbb{P}(\#\mathrm{T}(c)\leqslant A)
and
\mathrm{Var}(n^{-1}N_{n}(A))\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, which by Tchebytchev inequality entails the convergence in probability in the corollary. The convergence of
n^{-1}N_{n}(A)
to
\alpha(c)
as
A\to\infty
follows from Theorem
4.3
. ∎

6.2  An easy giant via
\varepsilon
-cut

Fix a graph
\mathfrak{g}
with
n
vertices and
\varepsilon>0
. An
\varepsilon
-cut
is a partition of
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
into two subsets
A
and
B
of size (number of vertices)
\varepsilon n
and
(1-\varepsilon)n
so that there is no edge between
A
and
B
in
\mathfrak{g}
. That notion was already used in the proof of Lemma
5.3
. The following deterministic lemma relates the existence of
\varepsilon
-cuts to the size of the largest component:

Lemma 6.6

(Giants makes cutting difficult)
.

Recall that
\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}(\mathfrak{g})
is the size of the largest component in
\mathfrak{g}
. Then one can find an
\varepsilon
-cut in
\mathfrak{g}
with

\left|\varepsilon-\frac{1}{2}\right|\leqslant\frac{1}{2}\frac{\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}(\mathfrak{g})}{n}.

Proof.
Let us put
x_{i}=n^{-1}\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{i}(\mathfrak{g})
for the renormalized cluster sizes in
\mathfrak{g}
ranked in non-increasing order so that
\sum_{i\geqslant 1}x_{i}=1
. Let

\ell=\inf\left\{j\geqslant 1:\sum_{i=1}^{j}x_{i}\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\right\}.

Since
x_{\ell}\leqslant x_{1}=\frac{\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}(\mathfrak{g})}{n}
we deduce that either
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell-1}x_{i}
or
\sum_{i=1}^{\ell}x_{i}
is
\frac{x_{1}}{2}
-close to
1/2
. Regrouping the vertices of the corresponding components, we get the desired
\varepsilon
-cut. ∎

An easy giant.

Let us use this lemma to quickly prove that there is a large component in
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
when
c>4\log 2
(this is a much weaker statement compared to Theorem
6.1
). Indeed, one can upper-bound the expected number of
\varepsilon
-cuts in
G(n,p)
by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\#\varepsilon-\mbox{cuts in }G(n,p)\right]\leqslant{n\choose\varepsilon n}(1-p)^{\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)n^{2}}\underset{p=\frac{c}{n}}{\leqslant}2^{n}\mathrm{exp}(-c\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)n).

(6.2)

When
c>4\log 2
the right-hand side tends exponentially fast to
0
as soon as
\varepsilon\in(\frac{1-\delta_{c}}{2},\frac{1+\delta_{c}}{2})
where
(\delta_{c})^{2}={1-\frac{4\log 2}{c}}
. Summing over all the at most
n
possible values of
\varepsilon
in this range, we deduce by the first moment method that for all
\eta>0
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\exists\ \varepsilon-\mbox{cut in }G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})\mbox{ with }\left|\frac{1}{2}-\varepsilon\right|\leqslant\frac{\delta_{c}-\eta}{2}\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}0,

hence by the above lemma

\quad\mathbb{P}\left(\exists\mbox{ a cluster of size at least }(\delta_{c}-\eta)\cdot n\mbox{ in }G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}1.

The former reasoning becomes very useful when we already start from a graph having large clusters: Suppose that
\mathfrak{g}\in\mathbb{G}_{N}
is a graph having only clusters of size
A>0
and denote by
G(N,p)\cup\mathfrak{g}
the graph obtained by superimposing it with an independent Erdős–Rényi random graph (and deleting the possible multiple edges). Then we have:

Lemma 6.7

(Sprinkling)
.

Fix
\delta,\varepsilon>0
and
A\in\{1,2,\dots\}
. The graph
G(N,\frac{\delta}{N})\cup\mathfrak{g}
has a giant component of size at least
(1-2\varepsilon)N
with high probability as
N\to\infty
as soon as we have

-\delta\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)+\frac{\log 2}{A}<0.

Proof.
As above, we compute the expected number of
\varepsilon
-cuts in
G(N,\frac{\delta}{N})\cup\mathfrak{g}
. Since those cuts have to be compatible with the initial structure of
\mathfrak{g}
, there are at most
2^{K}
choices where
K\leqslant N/A
is the number of connected components of
\mathfrak{g}
. Hence, the expected number of
\varepsilon
-cuts is upper bounded by

\mathbb{E}\left[\#\varepsilon-\mbox{cuts in }G(N,{\textstyle\frac{\delta}{n}})\cup\mathfrak{g}\right]\leqslant 2^{N/A}\left(1-\frac{\delta}{N}\right)^{\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)N^{2}},

and we conclude as above by the first moment method after summing over the at most
N
possible values of
\varepsilon
. ∎

Exercise 6.1
.

Suppose
n
is even. Use Theorem
6.2
to prove that the existence of a
\frac{1}{2}
-cut (i.e. a partition of the vertices into two subsets of the same cardinality without edges between them) in
G(n,p)
has a sharp threshold at

p_{n}=\frac{\log 4}{n}.

6.3  Sprinkling

We now gather Corollary
6.5
and Lemma
6.7
and prove Theorem
6.2
. The idea is to remark that the superimposition of two independent Erdős–Rényi random graph is again an Erdős–Rényi graph: for
c>1
and
\delta>0
we have that

\displaystyle G\big(n,\frac{c}{n}\big)\underset{\mathrm{indpt}}{\bigcup}G(n,\frac{\delta}{n})\quad\overset{(d)}{=}\quad G\Big(n,1-\big(1-\frac{c}{n}\big)\big(1-\frac{\delta}{n}\big)\Big)\quad\sqsubset\quad G\big(n,\frac{c+2\delta}{n}\big)

(6.3)

for
n
large enough.

Proof of Theorem
6.2
.
Fix
c>1
, fix
\varepsilon,\delta>0
small and
A>0
large. Denote by
\mathfrak{g}
the subgraph of
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
spanned by the vertices in components of size larger than
A
. We know from Corollary
6.5
that
\mathfrak{g}
is of size
N=n\mathbb{P}(\#\mathrm{T}(c)\geqslant A)+o_{\mathbb{P}}(n)
and we assume that
A
is large enough so that

\mathbb{P}(\#\mathrm{T}(c)\geqslant A)\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)(1-\alpha(c)),

in particular we used here that
c>1
so that
1-\alpha(c)>0
. Conditionally
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
and in particular on
\mathfrak{g}
and
N
, when
N\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)^{2}n
we can apply Lemma
6.7
and deduce that in the graph
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{\delta}{n}})\cup\mathfrak{g}
restricted to the vertices of
\mathfrak{g}
, there is w.h.p. a component of size at least
(1-\varepsilon)N
as soon as

\delta(1-\varepsilon)^{2}\varepsilon(1-\varepsilon)-\log 2/A>0.

Up to further increasing
A
, we can suppose that the former inequality is satisfied. We deduce that w.h.p. there is a component of size
(1-\varepsilon)N\geqslant(1-\varepsilon)^{3}(1-\alpha(c))+o_{\mathbb{P}}(n)
inside
G\big(n,\frac{c}{n}\big){\bigcup}G(n,\frac{\delta}{n})\sqsubset G\big(n,\frac{c+2\delta}{n}\big)
by (
6.3
). Letting
A\to\infty
while
\varepsilon\to 0
, this shows the existence of a connected component of size
at least

(1-\alpha(c))n+o_{\mathbb{P}}(n)
in
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c^{\prime}}{n}})
for any
c^{\prime}>c
. By continuity of
c\mapsto\alpha(c)
we deduce the existence of a connected component of size
at least

(1-\alpha(c))n+o_{\mathbb{P}}(n)
in
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
whereas Corollary
6.5
entails that
\alpha(c)n+o_{\mathbb{P}}(n)
of its vertices are in components of bounded size (irrespectively of
n
). This proves Theorem
6.2
.∎

Bibliographical notes.

The analysis of the phase transition for the emergence of the giant component is a classic in nowadays probability theory, see
[
53
]
for the initial paper and
[
68
]
and
[
10
]
for a detailed analysis. The proof of Section
6
is directly inspired by the original proof of Erdős and Rényi. The local limit paradigm is quite recent
[
21
]
and has been a fruitful idea applied in the realm of random graphs, see
[
11
,
19
]
for references.

Hints for Exercises.

Exercise
6.1
: At
p_{n}=\frac{\log 4}{n}
the giant component in
G(n,p_{n})
is of size
n/2+o_{\mathbb{P}}(n)
. A sharper result is even proved in
[
87
]
.

VII
:
Birth of a giant
2
, exploration and fluid limit

We now turn to a more modern and powerful way of proving Theorem
6.1
based on exploration techniques and stochastic analysis. We define an exploration process of
G(n,p)
which discovers its connected components one after the other in a Markovian way, by revealing its vertices one by one as
k=0,1,2,\dots,n
and study associated
\mathbb{R}
-valued Markov processes in the scaling limits. This will be the occasion to introduce the differential equation method, or fluid limit method whose applications are numerous.

7.1  Exploration process as a Markov chain

To properly define the exploration, we shall split the vertices
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
of
G(n,p)
into three categories: the
untouched
vertices
\mathcal{U}_{k}
, the
explored
vertices
\mathcal{E}_{k}
and the vertices in the current
stack

\mathcal{S}_{k}
whose neighborhoods remain to be explored. The algorithm evolves as follows:

•

at time
k=0
we have
\mathcal{E}_{0}=\varnothing
, the untouched vertices are
\mathcal{U}_{0}=\{2,3,\dots\}
and the only vertex in the stack is
\mathcal{S}_{1}=\{1\}
.

•

suppose
k\geqslant 0
is given and such that
\mathcal{S}_{k}\neq\varnothing
. We then select the vertex
x\in\mathcal{S}_{k}
with minimal label (recall that the vertex set of
G(n,p)
is
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
) and reveal all the neighbors
y_{1},\dots,y_{j}
of
x
among
\mathcal{U}_{k}
(this could be an empty set!). We then put

\mathcal{U}_{k+1}=\mathcal{U}_{k}\backslash\{y_{1},\dots,y_{j}\},\quad\mathcal{S}_{k+1}=\big(\mathcal{S}_{k}\backslash\{x\}\big)\cup\{y_{1},\dots,y_{j}\},\quad\mathcal{E}_{k+1}=\mathcal{E}_{k}\cup\{x\}.

•

When the current stack is empty
\mathcal{S}_{k}=\varnothing
then the
first stage
of the algorithm ends.

It should be clear from the above exploration that at time
\tau_{1}
when the first stage ends, the set of explored vertices
\mathcal{E}_{\tau_{1}}
is precisely the connected component of
1
in
G(n,p)
. If the graph is not yet entirely discovered, we shall continue the exploration in the remaining graph starting from the vertex with minimal label and consider the
Łukasiewicz path

(\mathbb{S}_{k}:0\leqslant k\leqslant n)

obtained by starting from
0
and whose increments are equal to the number of neighbors discovered in the untouched part minus
1
, see Figure
7.1
for an illustration. In terms of the stack process
\mathcal{S}_{k}
, this consists in immediately adding the vertex with minimal label yet untouched (as long as there are some untouched vertices left) when
\mathcal{S}_{k}
becomes empty (without performing a time step). In particular, the stack becomes empty if and only if the graph has been entirely explored.

Figure 7.1:
The Łukasiewicz exploration of a random graph. The edges revealed during the exploration are in thick lines, they form spanning trees of each components. The concatenation (ordered by the minimal label of their component) of the Łukasiewicz paths associated to those trees (explored by order of their labels) is the Łukasiewicz path of the graph.

Note that the excursions above the running infimum of
\mathbb{S}
correspond to the explorations of the different connected components of the graph, and in particular, if we introduce the running infimum process
\underline{\mathbb{S}}_{k}=\inf_{0\leqslant j\leqslant k}\mathbb{S}_{j}
then we can recover the size of the current stack
\#\mathcal{S}_{k}
as being

\displaystyle\#\mathcal{S}_{k}=\mathbb{S}_{k}-\underline{\mathbb{S}}_{k}+1,\quad\mbox{for }0\leqslant k\leqslant n-1.

(7.1)

We shall denote by
\mathcal{F}_{k}
for
k=0,\dots,n
the filtration generated by the first
k
steps of this exploration.

Proposition 7.1

(Markov property of the exploration)
.

For any
0\leqslant k\leqslant n
, conditionally on
(\mathcal{U}_{k},\mathcal{E}_{k},\mathcal{S}_{k})
, each edge in
G(n,p)
between
x
and
y
where
x,y\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
or
x\in\mathcal{U}_{k}
and
y\in\mathcal{S}_{k}
is present independently with probability
p
.

Proof.
Fix
k\geqslant 0
and notice that given the status of the edges and vertices revealed by time
k
, one could deterministically change the status of all the edges between
\mathcal{S}_{k}
and
\mathcal{U}_{k}
or in-between vertices of
\mathcal{U}_{k}
and this would not have affected the exploration up to time
k
(because these edges have not been explored by the algorithm). It is easy to see from this that those edges are indeed i.i.d. present with probability
p
.

An alternative, and more “algorithmic” way to see this is to imagine that all the edges of the graph
G(n,p)
carry a question mark “?” which means that its status is currently unknown, present with probability
p
and absent with probability
1-p
. When performing the exploration of the successive clusters, we reveal the status of certain edges (the question marks then disappear). The key point is to notice that since we are not allowed to use the randomness of unrevealed edges, at time
k
, conditionally on the past exploration, all the edges in question in the proposition still carry their “?” and so they are i.i.d. present with probability
p
and absent otherwise.∎

We deduce from the above that the process
\mathbb{S}
evolves in a Markovian fashion, if one also records its running infimum process:

Proposition 7.2
.

For
0\leqslant k\leqslant n-1
, conditionally on
\mathbb{S}_{0},\dots,\mathbb{S}_{k}
the increment
\Delta\mathbb{S}_{k}:=\mathbb{S}_{k+1}-\mathbb{S}_{k}
is distributed as

\Delta\mathbb{S}_{k}\overset{(d)}{=}\mathrm{Bin}(\#\mathcal{U}_{k},p)-1=\mathrm{Bin}(n-k-(\mathbb{S}_{k}-\underline{\mathbb{S}}_{k}+1),p)-1.

Proof.
This follows from the previous proposition, since the size of the stack
\mathcal{S}_{k}=\mathbb{S}_{k}-\underline{\mathbb{S}}_{k}+1
is given by (
7.1
) and since the number of untouched vertices is
n-k
minus the size of the current stack. ∎

7.2  Differential equation method or fluid limit

Fix
c>0
. In the rest of this section we take

p=\frac{c}{n}.

Taking expectations in Proposition
7.2
, according to a general principle that goes under the name of “
fluid limit
” or “
differential equation method
”, we anticipate that the process
\mathbb{S}
behaves in the large scale limit as a deterministic function
f_{c}
which satisfies the differential equation

\displaystyle f^{\prime}_{c}(t)=c\left(1-t-\big(f_{c}(t)-\underline{f_{c}}(t)\big)\right)-1,

(7.2)

and starts at
f_{c}(0)=0
, where we used the notation
\underline{g}(s)=\inf\{g(u):0\leqslant u\leqslant s\}
for a continuous function
g
. This is not a standard differential equation due to the seemingly awkward dependence in the function
\underline{f_{c}}
, but it is easy to convince oneself that the equation indeed has a unique solution and that this solution is either decreasing (when
c<1
) or unimodal (when
c>1
). More precisely:

For
\mathbf{c>1}
the function
f_{c}
is equivalently defined as the solution to the differential equation

f_{c}^{\prime}(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{lcl}c(1-t-f_{c}(t))-1&\mbox{for }&0\leqslant t<\inf\{s>0:f_{c}(s)=0\}\\
c(1-t)-1&\mbox{for }&\inf\{s>0:f_{c}(s)=0\}\leqslant t\leqslant 1.\end{array}\right.

In particular
f_{c}(t)=1-\mathrm{e}^{-ct}-t
until it comes back to
0
at time
1-\alpha(c)
where we recall from (
6.1
) that
\alpha\equiv\alpha(c)
is the solution in
(0,1)
to
\alpha=\mathrm{e}^{-c(1-\alpha)}.
For
t\geqslant(1-\alpha)
the function follows the parabola

f_{c}(t)=\frac{1}{2}(c(1+\alpha-t)-2)(t-1+\alpha).

Although
f_{c}
is
\mathcal{C}^{1}
over
[0,1]
, it is not
\mathcal{C}^{2}
at the point
1-\alpha(c)
since its second derivative jumps from
-\alpha c^{2}
to
-c
, see Figure
7.2
.

For
\mathbf{c\leqslant 1}
, since
f_{c}
is decreasing we have
\underline{f_{c}}(t)=f_{c}(t)
. It follows that we have
\alpha=1
and always have
f^{\prime}_{c}(t)=c(1-t)-1
so that

f_{c}(t)=\frac{t}{2}(2c-2-ct),\quad\forall t\in[0,1].

Figure 7.2:
Plot of the function
f_{2}
: it follows the orange curve from
0
to
1-\alpha(2)\approx 0.797
and then the blue curve from
1-\alpha(2)
to
1
. In particular, the function is not smooth at
t=1-\alpha(2)
.

The above heuristic is indeed correct and we have:

Theorem 7.3

(Fluid limit for the exploration)
.

Fix
c>0
and let
p=\frac{c}{n}
. Consider the Łukasiewicz exploration
(\mathbb{S}_{k}:0\leqslant k\leqslant n)
of the random graph
G(n,p)
. Then we have the following convergence in probability

\big(n^{-1}\cdot\mathbb{S}_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}:t\in[0,1]\big)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\big(f_{c}(t):t\in[0,1]\big),

for the uniform norm.

Proof.
Fix
c>0
. The idea of the fluid limit theorem is to argue that
\mathbb{S}_{k}
evolves as a stochastic Euler’s scheme based on the equation (
7.2
). To be more precise, we shall compare
\mathbb{S}_{k}
with the discrete function
\mathcal{L}_{k}=nf_{c}(\frac{k}{n})
for
k=0,1,2,\dots,n
. We shall write
\underline{\mathbb{S}}_{k}:=\inf\{\mathbb{S}_{j}:0\leqslant j\leqslant k\}
and
\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{k}:=\inf\{\mathcal{L}_{j}:0\leqslant j\leqslant k\}
for the running infimum processes of
\mathbb{S}
and
\mathcal{L}
respectively. First, from (
7.2
) and the fact that
f^{\prime}_{c}
is Lipschitz, it follows that we have the following Taylor approximation:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{k+1}-\mathcal{L}_{k}=\int_{k}^{k+1}\mathrm{d}s\,f^{\prime}_{c}\left(\frac{s}{n}\right)\underset{\eqref{eq:diffinf}}{=}c\left(1-\frac{k}{n}-\frac{\mathcal{L}_{k}}{n}+\frac{\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{k}}{n}\right)-1+\Theta(1/n),

(7.3)

where
\Theta(1/n)
is a function bounded in absolute value by
\mathrm{cst}/n
independently of
0\leqslant k\leqslant n
. We now analyse the process

X_{k}=\mathcal{L}_{k}-\mathbb{S}_{k}.

Writing
(\mathcal{F}_{k}:0\leqslant k\leqslant n)
for the filtration generated by the exploration, we first compute the expected conditional increment of the process
X
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[X_{k+1}-X_{k}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{diffF}\ \&\ \mathrm{Prop.}\ref{prop:markovER}}{=}

\displaystyle c\left(1-\frac{k}{n}-\frac{\mathcal{L}_{k}}{n}+\frac{\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{k}}{n}\right)-1+\Theta(1/n)

\displaystyle-\left(\mathbb{E}\left[\mathrm{Bin}\left(n-k-\mathbb{S}_{k}+\underline{\mathbb{S}}_{k}-1,\frac{c}{n}\right)\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}\right]-1\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle c\left(\frac{\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{k}-\underline{\mathbb{S}}_{k}}{n}-\frac{\mathcal{L}_{k}-\mathbb{S}_{k}}{n}\right)+\Theta(1/n).

Remark that
|\underline{\mathcal{L}}_{k}-\underline{\mathbb{S}}_{k}|\leqslant\sup_{0\leqslant i\leqslant k}|{\mathcal{L}}_{i}-{\mathbb{S}}_{i}|
so that taking absolute values in the last display we deduce that for all
0\leqslant k\leqslant n-1

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{E}[X_{k+1}-X_{k}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\frac{C}{n}\left(1+\sup_{0\leqslant i\leqslant k}|X_{i}|\right),

for some constant
C>0
. Furthermore, since the increments of
\mathbb{S}
are always stochastically dominated by
\mathrm{Bin}(n,\frac{c}{n})
it is plain to see that up to increasing
C
we have

\forall 0\leqslant k\leqslant n-1,\quad\mathbb{E}[(X_{k+1}-X_{k})^{2}]\leqslant C.

We are thus in position to apply the following “stochastic” version of Gronwall lemma to deduce that
n^{-1}\sup_{0\leqslant k\leqslant n}|X_{k}|\to 0
in probability. This entails the theorem.

7.2.1   Stochastic Gronwall lemma

Lemma 7.4

(Stochastic Gronwall lemma)
.

Let
(X_{k}:0\leqslant k\leqslant n)
be an adapted process with
X_{0}=0
. We define its supremum absolute value process
X_{k}^{*}=\sup\{|X_{j}|:0\leqslant j\leqslant k\}
for
0\leqslant k\leqslant n
and suppose that there exists
C>0
satisfying for all
0\leqslant k\leqslant n-1

•

|\mathbb{E}[X_{k+1}-X_{k}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]|\leqslant\frac{C}{n}\left(1+X_{k}^{*}\right)
almost surely,

•

\mathbb{E}[(X_{k+1}-X_{k})^{2}]\leqslant C
.

Then we have

n^{-1}\cdot X^{*}_{n}\to 0
in probability as
n\to\infty
.

Proof.
We decompose
X_{k}
in its predictable and its martingale part by putting for
0\leqslant k\leqslant n-1

X_{k+1}-X_{k}=\underbrace{\mathbb{E}[X_{k+1}-X_{k}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]}_{=:\ D_{k}}+\underbrace{((X_{k+1}-X_{k})-\mathbb{E}[X_{k+1}-X_{k}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}])}_{=:\ M_{k+1}-M_{k}},

so that if
M_{0}=0
then
(M_{k}:0\leqslant k\leqslant n)
is a martingale and

\displaystyle X_{k}=\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}D_{i}+M_{k}.

(7.4)

Let us first take care of the martingale part: We have by (the conditional) Jensen’s inequality

\mathbb{E}[(M_{k+1}-M_{k})^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{Var}(X_{k+1}-X_{k})\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]]\leqslant\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[(X_{k+1}-X_{k})^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]]=\mathbb{E}[(X_{k+1}-X_{k})^{2}].

Since the increments of a martingale are orthogonal in
L^{2}
by the above calculation we deduce that
\mathbb{E}[M_{n}^{2}]\leqslant 4Cn
. By Doob’s maximal inequality we have

\mathbb{P}(\sup_{0\leqslant k\leqslant n}|M_{k}|\geqslant A)\leqslant 4\frac{\mathbb{E}[|M_{n}|^{2}]}{A^{2}}

and it follows that

\frac{\sup_{0\leqslant k\leqslant n}|M_{k}|}{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}0.

The rest of the argument is purely deterministic. By the hypothesis in the proposition we have
D_{i}\leqslant\frac{C}{n}(X_{i}^{*}+1)
and so (
7.4
) combined with the fact that the martingale part is negligible in front of
n
yield that for any
\varepsilon>0
on the event
\{\sup_{0\leqslant k\leqslant n}|M_{k}|\leqslant\varepsilon n\}
, whose probability tends to
1
as
n\to\infty
, we have for all
t\in[0,n]

X_{[t]}^{*}\underset{\eqref{eq:decompositionDoob}}{\leqslant}\sum_{i=0}^{[t]-1}\frac{C}{n}X^{*}_{i}+\left(\varepsilon+\frac{C}{n}\right)n\leqslant 2\varepsilon n+\frac{C}{n}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,X^{*}_{[s]}.

On this event, by the usual (deterministic) Grönwall
1
1

1

Thomas Hakon Grönwall (1877–1932), Swedish
lemma we have
X^{*}_{[t]}\leqslant 2\varepsilon n\cdot\mathrm{exp}(\frac{C}{n}t),
and in particular
n^{-1}\cdot X^{*}_{n}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
in probability. ∎

The above strategy, called the “differential equation method” by Wormald
[
118
]
, has found many applications in the realm of random graphs. Rather than giving an abstract convergence theorem, we propose to apply the strategy in the following exercise in order to estimate the size of the (random) greedy independent set on an Erdős–Rényi random graph:

Exercise 7.1

(Greedy independent set on
G(n,p)
)
.

Consider the graph
G(n,p)
over the vertices
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
for a parameter
p=\frac{c}{n}
for some constant
c>0
. We will build inductively a random subset
\mathbf{I}
of
G(n,p)
so that no vertices of
\mathbf{I}
are neighbors. To do this we put initially
\mathcal{U}_{0}=\{1,2,3,\dots.,n\}
(untouched) and
\mathbf{I}_{0}=\varnothing
. Iteratively, for
i\geqslant 0
as long as
\mathcal{U}_{i}\neq\varnothing
we select the vertex
x_{i}
of smallest label in
\mathcal{U}_{i}
and denote its neighbors in
G(n,p)
by
\{y_{1},\dots,y_{j}\}
we then put

\mathbf{I}_{i+1}=\mathbf{I}_{i}\cup\{x_{i}\}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathcal{U}_{i+1}=\mathcal{U}_{i}\backslash\{x_{i},y_{1},\dots,y_{j}\}.

We denote by
\mathcal{F}_{n}
the canonical filtration generated by this process and consider the stopping time

\tau=\inf\{k\geqslant 0:\mathcal{U}_{k}=\varnothing\}.

1.

Show that
\mathbf{I}_{\tau}
is an independent set, that is, no vertices of
\mathbf{I}_{\tau}
are neighbors.

2.

Show that conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{k}
, the graph induced by
G(n,p)
on
\mathcal{U}_{k}
is an Erdős-Rényi random graph with parameter
p
. That is, all edges between vertices of
\mathcal{U}_{k}
are independent and present with probability
p
.

3.

Deduce that

\mathbb{E}[\#\mathcal{U}_{k+1}-\#\mathcal{U}_{k}\mid\mathcal{F}_{k}]=-1-(\#\mathcal{U}_{k}-1)p.

4.

Recall that
p=p_{n}=\frac{c}{n}
. Use the differential equation method to prove that

(n^{-1}\#\mathcal{U}_{\lfloor nt\rfloor})_{t\in[0,1]}\to\left(\frac{(1+c-\mathrm{e}^{{ct}})\mathrm{e}^{{-ct}}}{c}\vee 0:0\leqslant t\leqslant 1\right).

Hint :
f(t)=\frac{(1+c-\mathrm{e}^{{ct}})\mathrm{e}^{{-ct}}}{c}
satisfies
f^{\prime}(t)=-1-cf(t)
and
f(0)=1
.

5.

Deduce and explain why

n^{-1}\tau\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}\frac{\log(1+c)}{c}.

7.3  Corollaries and refined estimates

Let us deduce some geometric consequences of the convergence of the rescaled process
\mathbb{S}
towards
f_{c}
(Theorem
7.3
). Recall from the definition of the exploration of the connected components of
G(n,p)
that:

1.

The number of components in
G(n,p)
is exactly
-\underline{\mathbb{S}}_{n}
,

2.

The sizes of the components in
G(n,p)
correspond to the lengths of the excursions of
\mathbb{S}
above its running infimum
\underline{\mathbb{S}}
.

As a direct corollary of Theorem
7.3
and the first item above we deduce:

Corollary 7.5
.

The number of components in
G(n,p)
satisfies

\displaystyle\frac{\#\mathrm{ConnComp}(G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}}))}{n}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}

\displaystyle f_{c}(1)=\frac{\alpha(c)(2-c\alpha(c))}{2},\quad\mbox{ for }c>0.

If
f:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}
is a continuous function with
f(0)=0
and running infimum process
\underline{f}(t)=\inf_{0\leqslant s\leqslant t}f(s)
, we denote by
\mathrm{Exc}(f)
the (at most countably many) excursion intervals of
f-\underline{f}
away from
0
. We write
\|\mathrm{Exc}(f)\|\in\ell_{1}
for the lengths of those excursions ranked in decreasing order. We deduce the weak-giant property (Theorem
6.2
) from Exercise
7.2
and the continuous mapping theorem. In particular, as in Section
6
, we established the existence of the unique giant component but we did not give the logarithmic upper bounds for the second largest component stated in Theorem
6.1
. To prove it, we will use large deviations estimates in the next section.

Exercise 7.2
.

Consider the mapping

\mathcal{E}:f\in\big(\mathcal{C}([0,1],\mathbb{R}),\|\cdot\|_{\infty}\big)\mapsto\|\mathrm{Exc}(f)\|\in(\ell^{1},\|\cdot\|_{1}).

1.

Show that
\mathcal{E}
is not continuous in general.

2.

However, show that
\mathcal{E}
is continuous at points
f
where
f
has no two-sided local minima. In particular,
\mathcal{E}
is continuous at
f=f_{c}
for
c\geqslant 0
.

In the rest of this section, we prove most of the refined estimates on the cluster size stated in Theorem
6.1
, especially the logarithmic upper bound in the subcritical case and for the second largest cluster in the supercritical case. We start with a stochastic domination of the typical cluster size coming from the exploration process.

By Proposition
7.2
, since we always have
\#\mathcal{U}_{k}\leqslant n
, we deduce that the increments of
\mathbb{S}
are stochastically dominated by independent
\mathrm{Bin}(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
variables minus
1
. We denote by
(S^{(n)}_{t}:t\geqslant 0)
a random walk starting from
0
with i.i.d. increments of law
\mathrm{Bin}(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})-1
. In particular, the size of the cluster of
1
in
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
is stochastically dominated by
T_{-1}(S^{(n)})
, the hitting time of
-1
by that random walk. This can be evaluated via Kemperman’s formula (Proposition
3.5
) and an explicit computation:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(T_{-1}(S^{(n)})=k)

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{Kemperman}}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(k\cdot n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})=k-1)

(7.5)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}{nk\choose k-1}\left(\frac{c}{n}\right)^{k-1}\left(1-\frac{c}{n}\right)^{nk-(k-1)}

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{Stirling}}{\leqslant}

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cst}\cdot\frac{n}{k}\frac{\sqrt{nk}(nk)^{nk}}{\sqrt{k}k^{k-1}\sqrt{nk}((n-1)k)^{(n-1)k+1}}\left(\frac{c}{n}\left(1-\frac{c}{n}\right)^{n-1}\right)^{k}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cst}\cdot\frac{1}{k^{3/2}}\cdot\frac{(nk)^{nk}}{k^{k}((n-1)k)^{(n-1)k}}\left(\frac{c}{n}\left(1-\frac{c}{n}\right)^{n-1}\right)^{k}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cst}\cdot\frac{1}{k^{3/2}}\cdot\left(\frac{n^{n}}{(n-1)^{(n-1)}}\left(\frac{c}{n}\left(1-\frac{c}{n}\right)^{n-1}\right)\right)^{k}

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\mathrm{Cst}\cdot\frac{1}{k^{3/2}}\cdot\left(c\left(\frac{n-c}{n-1}\right)^{n-1}\right)^{k},

for some constant
\mathrm{Cst}>0
that may vary from line to line but which is independent of
k\geqslant 1
and
n\geqslant 2
. When
c<1
the term in the parenthesis tends to
c\mathrm{e}^{1-c}<1
as
n\to\infty
, whereas for
c=1
this term is equal to
1
.

7.3.1   Subcritical case

We can now prove the logarithmic upper bound on the size of the clusters in the subcritical regime in Theorem
6.1
: Suppose that
c<1
and recall that
\mathcal{C}^{i}\equiv\mathcal{C}^{i}(G(n,\frac{c}{n}))
is the cluster of the vertex
i\in\{1,2,3,\dots,n\}
inside
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
. Using the above bound, we deduce that for any
A>0
, we have in
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
:

\mathbb{P}(\#\mathcal{C}^{1}\geqslant A)\leqslant\mathbb{P}(T_{-1}(S^{(n)})\geqslant A)\underset{\eqref{eq:tailexact}}{\leqslant}\mathrm{Cst}\cdot\eta^{A},

where
\eta<1
is independent of
A
. Taking
A=\frac{2}{\eta}\log n
, we deduce using the union bound that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{C}_{1}^{\max}\geqslant\frac{2}{\eta}\log n)

\displaystyle\underset{\mathrm{union\ bd}}{\leqslant}

\displaystyle n\mathbb{P}\left(\#\mathcal{C}^{1}\geqslant\frac{2}{\eta}\log n\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle n\mathbb{P}(T_{-1}(S^{(n)})\geqslant\frac{2}{\eta}\log n)\leqslant\mathrm{Cst}\cdot n\exp(-2\log n)\to 0.

7.3.2   Critical case

The same strategy can be used in the critical case
c=1
(together with a little size-biasing trick). More precisely, imagine that we pick (independently of
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{1}{n}})
) a vertex
U_{n}
uniformly in
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
. The size of the cluster of
U_{n}
has the same law as that of the vertex
1
and so is stochastically dominated by
T_{-1}(S^{(n)})
. We can thus write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(T_{-1}(S^{(n)})\geqslant A)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\#\mathcal{C}^{U_{n}}\geqslant A)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\#\mathcal{C}_{\max}\geqslant A\mbox{ and }U_{n}\in\mathcal{C}_{\max})\geqslant\frac{A}{n}\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}\geqslant A).

Now taking
A=\lambda n^{2/3}
and using (
7.5
) where the exponential factor disappears when
c=1
, we find

\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}\geqslant\lambda n^{2/3})=O(\lambda^{-3/2}),

which already gives the good order of magnitude of the largest cluster in
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{1}{n}})
. Getting the full distributional convergence of
(n^{-2/3}\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{i})_{i\geqslant 1}
requires to understand in much more details the exploration process. See the next chapter for such a result (Proposition
8.5
) in a slight variant of the Erdős–Rényi random graph.

7.3.3   Supercritical case

For the supercritical case, we shall establish a common phenomenon in statistical physics: in the supercritical regime, the complement of the giant behaves as a
subcritical
system. See Exercise
7.3
for an instance of this phenomenon in BGW trees.

Let
c>1
. By Theorem
7.3
we know that after the giant component of
G(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
has been explored we are left with a graph over
\approx\alpha(c)n
vertices with edge density
\frac{c}{n}
. This graph is close to being an Erdős–Rényi:

Lemma 7.6
.

Conditionally on
\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}
and on
\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}>\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{2}
, the remaining graph
2
2

2

with vertices relabeled in increasing order

G(n,p)\backslash\mathcal{C}_{\max}
has law
G(n-\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1},p)
conditioned on having clusters of size strictly less than
\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}
.

Proof.
Fix a connected component
