\mathfrak{g}_{\max}
on
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and a given graph
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{rem}}
on the remaining vertices so that no component of
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{rem}}
has a cluster of size larger or equal to
\#\mathrm{V}(\mathfrak{g}_{\max})
. Then we have (with a slight abuse of notation)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{C}^{\max}=\mathfrak{g}_{\max}\mbox{ and }G(n,p)\backslash\mathcal{C}^{\max}=\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{rem}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle{p}^{\#\mathrm{E}(\mathfrak{g}_{\max})}p^{\#\mathrm{E}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{rem}})}(1-p)^{{n\choose 2}-\#\mathrm{E}(\mathfrak{g}_{\max})-\#\mathrm{E}(\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{rem}})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(G\big(n-\#\mathrm{V}(\mathfrak{g}_{\max}),p\big)=\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{rem}}\Big)\cdot\mathrm{Cst}(\mathfrak{g}_{\max}),

where the constant
\mathrm{Cst}(\mathfrak{g}_{\max})
only depends on
\mathfrak{g}_{\max}
and not on
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathrm{rem}}
as long as its components have size strictly less than
\#\mathrm{V}(\mathfrak{g}_{\max})
. This proves the lemma.

When
c>1
, notice that the complement of the giant component is
subcritical
since

\forall c>0,\qquad c\alpha(c)<1,

see Figure
7.3
.

Figure 7.3:
Plot of
c\mapsto c\,\alpha(c)
displaying the criticality in the remaining graph when the giant has been removed.

We can thus prove point (ii) in Theorem
6.1
: Fix
c>1
and
\alpha(c)/2>\varepsilon>0
. By Theorem
6.2
, the event

\{|\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}-(1-\alpha(c))n|\leqslant\varepsilon n\}\cap\{\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{2}<\varepsilon n\}

has a probability tending to
1
and conditionally on it, the complement of the giant
G(n,\frac{c}{n})\backslash\mathcal{C}^{\max}
is an Erdős–Rényi with
N\leqslant(\alpha(c)+\varepsilon)n
vertices and edge density
\frac{c}{n}
, conditioned on having no cluster of size larger than
\mathrm{C}^{\max}_{1}
. If
\varepsilon>0
is small enough so that
c(\alpha(c)+\varepsilon)<1
, we know from Section
7.3.1
that
G(N,\frac{c}{n})
has no cluster of size larger than
A\log n
for some
A>0
, so the previous conditioning does not affect its law asymptotically and we deduce (ii) in Theorem
6.1
.

Exercise 7.3

(Supercritical BGW conditioned to be finite are subcritical BGW)
.

Let
\mathcal{T}
be a BGW tree with a
supercritical
offspring distribution
\mu
with generating function
g
. We denote by
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}
the tree
\mathcal{T}
conditioned on the event
\{\#\mathcal{T}<\infty\}
whose probability is equal to the unique solution
\alpha\in(0,1)
to
g(\alpha)=\alpha
, see Figure
4.5
. Show that
\tilde{\mathcal{T}}
is a BGW with offspring distribution
\tilde{\mu}
whose generating function
\tilde{g}
is given by

\tilde{g}(z)=\frac{1}{\alpha}g(\alpha\cdot z),\quad\mbox{ for }z\in[0,1].

Bibliographical notes.

Although the exploration process of
G(n,p)
is well known (see e.g.
[
13
, (11.12)]
for Proposition
7.2
), the existence of the giant component using fluid limit for the exploration process seems to be new, although it is inspired by the much more precise analysis made in Aldous
[
10
]
and in
[
92
]
. More generally, the differential equation method has been used widely in random graph theory, see
[
118
]
. Many formulations of the fluid limit paradigm, with increasing level of generality, can be found in the literature, see e.g.
[
118
,
116
,
39
,
40
]
. Studying the emergence and the structure of the giant component in
G(n,p)
is still a vivid subject in probability theory, see e.g.
[
3
]
for very recent results relating the critical Erdős-Rényi graph to the minimal spanning tree or
[
101
]
for a a connection with mixing time for the composition of transpositions on the symmetric group. We refer to
[
113
]
for extensions and more references.

: The result is first proved in
[

]
.

: The mapping
\mathcal{E}
is not continuous at the function
f:x\mapsto|x||x-1/2||x-1|
as limit of the functions
f_{\varepsilon}:x\mapsto|x|\big(|x-1/2|+\varepsilon\big)|x-1|
.

. Use (
4.2
) and massage it. See Corollary 2.7 in
[

]
for a proof.

VIII
:
Birth of giant
3
, Poissonized

Pas frais mon poisson ? (Ordralfabétix)

Figure 8.1:
Ordralfabétix (© Goscinny et Uderzo).

We introduce a variant of the Erdős–Rényi random graph where infinitely “stack” vertices are added on the side. A very simple Markov property of the model entails that the Łukasiewicz exploration is made of simple increments related to the repartition function of i.i.d. uniforms. Using the standard Glivenko–Cantelli theorem, this enables us to give very short proofs of classical results such as the phase transition for the giant component (Theorem
6.1
) or the connectedness for the standard Erdős–Rényi model (Theorem
5.2
).

8.1  The stacked model and its exploration

We shall consider a variant of the Erdős–Rényi model where we add infinitely many additional vertices “in a stack on the side”. Formally, for
n\geqslant 1
and
p
fixed we consider the graph
\mathrm{G}^{\mathrm{stack}}(n,p)
on the vertex set
\{1,2,\dots,n\}\cup\{1^{*},2^{*},3^{*},\dots\}
, the vertices of
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
form the
core
of the graph, whereas the vertices
\{1^{*},2^{*},\dots\}
form the
stack
. Then, each pair of core and stack vertices are connected by an edge with probability
p
independently.
There are no edges between vertices of the stack
. See Figure
8.2
.

Figure 8.2:
A stacked Erdős–Rényi random graph and one step of exploration. The stack is made of the white vertices on the left part while the core is represented by the gray part. After one step of exploration, the explored vertex (in red) is deleted as well as the edges linking the discovered vertices between each other or to the former stack. Conditionally on the number
n^{\prime}
of vertices remaining in the core after this exploration, the resulting graph (after relabeling of its vertices) is distributed as
G^{\mathrm{stack}}(n^{\prime},p)
.

Markov property.

A step of
exploration
in
\mathrm{G}^{\mathrm{stack}}(n,p)
is the following: Fix a vertex
\rho
of the stack (independently of the core) and reveal its neighbors
y_{1},\dots,y_{K}
with
K\geqslant 0
inside the core. Then, see those vertices
y_{1},\dots,y_{K}
as new vertices of the stack, in particular erase all possible edges between
y_{1},\dots,y_{K}
and between
y_{1},\dots,y_{K}
and other vertices of the stack. Denote by
\mathrm{Explo}(\mathrm{G}^{\mathrm{stack}}(n,p))
the resulting random graph whose vertices are relabeled by
\{1,2,\dots,n-K\}
and
\{1^{*},2^{*},\dots\}
accordingly. The following is trivially verified:

Lemma 8.1

(Markov property of
\mathrm{G}^{\mathrm{stack}}(n,p)
)
.

Let
K\geqslant 0
be the number of neighbors in the core of
\mathrm{G}^{\mathrm{stack}}(n,p)
of the stack vertex
\rho
. Then
K\sim\mathrm{Bin}(n,p)
and conditionally on
K
, we have the equality in law

\mathrm{Explo}(\mathrm{G}^{\mathrm{stack}}(n,p))\quad\overset{(d)}{=}\mathrm{G}^{\mathrm{stack}}(n-K,p).

We shall now consider successive exploration steps and denote by
K\equiv K_{1},K_{2},\dots
the number of vertices of the remaining core discovered at each step. In the rest of the chapter, we shall focus on a specific exploration of the graph: we shall assume that iteratively, the discovered vertices are placed on top of the stack and that we successively explore the first vertex of the stack. We get the so-called
Łukasiewicz exploration
of the graph
\mathrm{G^{stack}}(n,p)
similar to the one used in the previous chapter, see Figure
8.3
. We encode it in a process

(\mathbb{S}_{k}^{(n,p)}:k\geqslant 0)

or in short
(\mathbb{S}_{k}:k\geqslant 0)
, the Łukasiewicz walk, defined by
\mathbb{S}^{(n,p)}_{0}=0
and where
\Delta\mathbb{S}^{(n,p)}_{i}=\mathbb{S}^{(n,p)}_{i}-\mathbb{S}^{(n,p)}_{i-1}=K_{i}-1
is the number of neighbors discovered at step
i
minus one.

Figure 8.3:
Łukasiewicz exploration of the graph
\mathrm{G^{stack}}(n,p)
: the numbering reflects the order in which the vertices have been explored. The thick edges are kept whereas the thin red edges are discarded in the exploration. The thick (and very thick) edges form
\mathrm{F^{stack}}(n,p)
and the very thick ones form
\mathrm{F^{\prime stack}}(n,p)
. The process
\mathbb{S}^{(n,p)}
on the right is obtained by concatenating the successive number of neighbors
-1
.

Relation to components.

Since
\mathrm{G^{stack}}(n,p)
has an infinite stack of vertices linked to each vertex of the core independently with probability
p
, as soon as
p>0
, the graph is a.s. connected and in fact all vertices of the core have infinite degree almost surely. However, if we only consider the edges that are truly used in the Łukasiewicz exploration (i.e. not the edges between stack and revealed vertices, nor edges between revealed vertices) we obtain a spanning forest

\mathrm{F^{\mathrm{stack}}}(n,p)\sqsubset\mathrm{G^{\mathrm{stack}}}(n,p),

whose Łukasiewciz walk is precisely
\mathbb{S}
,
see Figure
8.3
. In particular, new minimal records of
\mathbb{S}
correspond to the discovery of a new tree component in
\mathrm{F^{\mathrm{stack}}}(n,p)
. If we further remove all vertices of the initial stack (together with the adjacent edges) we split
\mathrm{F^{\mathrm{stack}}}(n,p)
into a finer forest
\mathrm{F^{\mathrm{stack},^{\prime}}}(n,p)
which spans the core and we can check the following graph inclusions

\displaystyle\mathrm{F^{\mathrm{stack},^{\prime}}}(n,p)\sqsubset\underbrace{G(n,p)}_{\mathrm{Core}}\sqsubset\mathrm{F^{\mathrm{stack}}}(n,p)\sqsubset\mathrm{G^{\mathrm{stack}}}(n,p).

(8.1)

8.2  Law of the increments

The advantage of the stacked version compared to the standard Erdős–Rényi studied in the previous chapter is that the law of the increments of
\mathbb{S}
is simpler as it does not involved the running infimum process (compare Proposition
8.2
with Proposition
7.2
). To make it even simpler, it is useful to randomize the size of the core. We first start with the description of
(\Delta\mathbb{S}^{(n,p)}_{k}:k\geqslant 1)
in the fixed-size case.

8.2.1   Fixed size

Consider the unit interval
[0,1)
which is split in infinitely many subintervals

[0,1)=\bigsqcup_{k\geqslant 1}\underbrace{\big[x^{(p)}_{k-1},x^{(p)}_{k}\big[}_{:=I^{(p)}_{k}},\quad\mbox{ where }x^{(p)}_{k}=1-\left(1-p\right)^{k}\mbox{ for }k\geqslant 0,

so that for each
k\geqslant 1
, the length of
I^{(p)}_{k}
is exactly
p
times the total length of
I^{(p)}_{k},I^{(p)}_{k+1},\dots
. We then throw
(U_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant n)
independent identically distributed uniform r.v. on
[0,1]
. The observation is:

Lemma 8.2
.

The law of
(\Delta\mathbb{S}^{(n,p)}_{k}+1:k\geqslant 1)
is equal to the law of

\big(\#\{1\leqslant i\leqslant n:U_{i}\in I_{k}^{(p)}\}\big)_{k\geqslant 1}.

Proof
. Denote by
\tilde{K}_{j}=\#\{1\leqslant i\leqslant n:U_{i}\in I_{j}^{(p)}\}
. Clearly
\tilde{K}_{1}\sim\mathrm{Bin}(n,p)
in law. Furthermore, using the fact that the variables are uniform, we see that conditionally on
\tilde{K}_{1}
, the sequence
\tilde{K}_{2},\tilde{K}_{3},\dots
has the law of
(\tilde{K}_{1},\tilde{K}_{2},\dots)
where
n
has been replaced by
n^{\prime}=n-\tilde{K}_{1}
. Comparing with Lemma
8.1
this suffices to prove equality of the laws recursively. ∎

If we write
F_{n}(x)=\#\{1\leqslant i\leqslant n:U_{i}\leqslant x\}
for the repartition function of the
n
i.i.d. uniforms, using the above proposition we can write
simultaneously
for all
k\geqslant 0

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle F_{n}(x_{k}^{(p)})-k.

(8.2)

For our application, we recall the classical Glivenko-Cantelli

1
1

1

Valery Ivanovich Glivenko (1897–1940), Ukrainian
Francesco Paolo Cantelli (1875 – 1966) Italian

theorem:

\displaystyle\left(\frac{F_{n}(x)}{n}:x\in[0,1]\right)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}

\displaystyle(x:x\in[0,1]),

(8.3)

for the
L^{\infty}
metric. Before drawing probabilistic consequences of the above observations, let us consider the model where the size of the core is itself random which yield to further simplifications (and which gave the name to the chapter).

8.2.2   Poissonized version

Fix
\alpha>0
and suppose that
n\equiv N
is first sampled at random, with law
\mathfrak{P}(\alpha)
and conditionally on
N
we perform the above construction. The resulting stacked graph will be denoted by
\mathrm{G_{Poi}^{stack}}(\alpha,p)
and we denote the resulting Łukasiewicz walk by
\mathbb{S}^{[\alpha,p]}
. By the classical Poisson thinning observation, in Lemma
8.1
we then have
K\sim\mathrm{Bin}(N,p)\sim\mathfrak{P}(\alpha p)
and furthermore
K
is independent of
N-K\sim\mathfrak{P}((1-p)\alpha)
. Iterating the above lemma, we deduce that in the Poissonized version the increments
\Delta\mathbb{S}^{[\alpha,p]}_{k}+1
of the Łukasiewicz walk is now a sequence of
independent
Poisson random variables with expectation
\alpha p,\alpha p(1-p),\dots,\alpha p(1-p)^{k},\dots
whose total sum is just a Poisson variable of parameter
\alpha p\sum_{i\geqslant 0}(1-p)^{i}=\alpha
, recovering the total number of vertices
N
in the core as expected.

As in (
8.2
) we can write in this case
simultaneously
for all
k\geqslant 0

\displaystyle\mathbb{S}^{[\alpha,p]}_{k}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{P}(\alpha p)-1)+(\mathfrak{P}(\alpha p(1-p))-1)+\dots+(\mathfrak{P}(\alpha p(1-p)^{k-1})-1)

(8.4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathfrak{P}\left(\alpha p\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{k-1}(1-p)^{i}\right)-k=\mathfrak{P}\left(\alpha(1-(1-p)^{k})\right)-k,

where all the Poisson random variables written above are independent and where
(\mathfrak{P}(t):t\geqslant 0)
is a standard unit-rate Poisson counting process on
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. We shall only use the following standard limit theorems on the Poisson counting process

\displaystyle\frac{\mathfrak{P}(t)}{t}\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{a.s.}1,\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\frac{(\mathfrak{P}(tn)-tn)}{\sqrt{n}}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(d)}(B_{t}:t\geqslant 0),

(8.5)

where
(B_{t}:t\geqslant 0)
is a standard linear Brownian motion. The left-hand side follows from the law of large numbers and the right-hand side from Donsker’s invariance principle.

8.3  Phase transition for the giant

Let us use the Łukasiewicz exploration of the stacked version of the Erdős–Rényi random graph to give a straightforward proof of Theorem
6.2
.

8.3.1   Existence of the giant component

Fix
c>0
. Let
p\equiv p_{n}=\frac{c}{n}
and recall the notation
\mathbb{S}^{(n,p)}
for the Łukasiewicz walk encoding the fixed size stacked Erdős–Rényi random graph. Since we have

x_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}^{(\frac{c}{n})}\sim\left(1-\frac{c}{n}\right)^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\to 1-\mathrm{e}^{-ct},\quad\mbox{ as $n\to\infty$ uniformly over $t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}$},

using (
8.2
) and the Glivenko-Cantelli theorem (
8.3
), we immediately deduce the analog of Theorem
7.3
:

Proposition 8.3

(Fluid limit)
.

We have the following convergences in probability

\sup_{t\geqslant 0}\left\|\left(n^{-1}\cdot{\mathbb{S}^{(n,\frac{c}{n})}_{\lfloor nt\rfloor}}\right)-\left(1-\mathrm{e}^{{-ct}}-t\right)\right\|\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}0.

Figure 8.4:
Graphs of the functions
(1-\mathrm{e}^{{-ct}}-t)_{t\geqslant 0}
for different of values of
c
: in blue
c=1/2
, in orange
c=1
, in green
c=2
and in red
c=3
. Notice the root
1-\alpha(c)
and compare with Figure
7.2
.

Notice that the above convergence is not restricted to a compact time interval compared to Theorem
7.3
. However, when
c>1
, the function
t\mapsto 1-\mathrm{e}^{-ct}-t
coincides with the function
f_{c}
defined in Section
7.2
up to its first root at time
t=1-\alpha(c)
, where we recall that
\alpha(c)
is the smallest root to
\alpha(c)=\mathrm{e}^{-c(1-\alpha(c))}
and in particular
\alpha(c)=1
if and only if
c\in[0,1]
. We give a proof of the existence of the giant component in the Poissonized Erdős–Rényi (that is Theorem
6.2
) using the same lines as in Section
7.3
:

Corollary 8.4

(Phase transition for
G(n,\frac{c}{n})
)
.

If
c<1
then the largest connected components
in the core
of
\mathrm{G^{stack}}(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
has size
o_{\mathbb{P}}(n)
, whereas if
c>1
it contains a unique giant component of size
(1-\alpha(c))n+o_{\mathbb{P}}(n)
, and the second largest component has size
o_{\mathbb{P}}(n)
.

Proof.
Using the sandwiching of (
8.1
) it suffices to prove the similar statements for
\mathrm{F^{stack}}
and
\mathrm{F^{stack,^{\prime}}}
. The size of the connected components in
\mathrm{F^{stack}}(n,\frac{c}{n})
are given by the lengths of the excursions of
\mathbb{S}^{(n,\frac{c}{n})}
above its running infimum process

\underline{\mathbb{S}}^{(n,\frac{c}{n})}_{k}:=\inf\{\mathbb{S}^{(n,\frac{c}{n})}_{j}:0\leqslant j\leqslant k\}.

We denote by
(L^{(n,\frac{c}{n})}_{i}:i\geqslant 1)
those excursion lengths ranked in decreasing order. Notice that the excursion lengths above the running infimum of the function
t\mapsto 1-\mathrm{e}^{-ct}-t
are given by
(1-\alpha(c),0,0,\dots)
. Using Proposition
8.3
and (a variation on) Exercise
7.2
shows that

\left(\frac{L^{(n,\frac{c}{n})}_{i}}{n}:i\geqslant 1\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}(1-\alpha(c),0,0,\dots)

for the
\ell^{\infty}
norm. This proves the statement of the corollary for the random graph
\mathrm{F^{\mathrm{stack}}}(n,{\textstyle\frac{c}{n}})
. In the case
c\leqslant 1
, since
\mathrm{F^{stack,^{\prime}}}\sqsubset\mathrm{F^{stack}}
and
1-\alpha(c)=0
there is nothing more to prove. However, when
c>1
the removal of the initial stack vertices may split the giant component of
\mathrm{F^{stack}}(n,\frac{c}{n})
of size
(1-\alpha(c))n+o_{\mathbb{P}}(n)
into several components but a moment’s though using the Łukasiewicz walk and Proposition
8.3
again shows that one component of size
(1-\alpha(c))n+o_{\mathbb{P}}(n)
must remain.∎

8.3.2   Critical case

In this section we turn to refined estimates on the cluster sizes in the case
\alpha=n
and
p\equiv p_{n}=\frac{1}{n}
. For technical simplicity, we focus on the Poissonized version
\mathrm{G^{stack}_{Poi}}
for which we can use the Brownian limit in (
8.5
). This is an analog of point (iii) in Theorem
6.1
(where we take
\lambda=0
below). Getting from those results the analogs for the fixed-size Erdős–Rényi via depoissonization is doable, but is not covered in these pages.

Proposition 8.5

(Near critical case)
.

Fix
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
. For
p\equiv p_{n}=\frac{1}{n}+\frac{\lambda}{n^{{4/3}}}
with
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, the Łukasiewicz walk
\mathbb{S}^{[n,\frac{1}{n}+\frac{\lambda}{n^{4/3}}]}
of the Poissonized version satisfies

\left(n^{-1/3}\cdot{\mathbb{S}^{[n,\frac{1}{n}+\frac{\lambda}{n^{4/3}}]}_{\lfloor n^{2/3}t\rfloor}}\right)_{t\geqslant 0}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(d)}\left(B_{t}+\lambda t-\frac{t^{2}}{2}\right)_{t\geqslant 0},

where the convergence holds in distribution for the uniform norm over every compact of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
.

Proof.
Fix
A>0
. Putting
k=\lfloor n^{2/3}t\rfloor
for
t\in[0,A]
in the equation (
8.4
), we have

\displaystyle n\left(1-(1-\frac{1}{n}-\frac{\lambda}{n^{4/3}})^{\lfloor n^{2/3}t\rfloor}\right)=tn^{2/3}+\lambda tn^{1/3}-\frac{t^{2}}{2}n^{1/3}+o(n^{1/3}),

(8.6)

as
n\to\infty
and where the little
o
is uniform in
t\in[0,A]
. The second item of (
8.5
) together with Skorokhod representation theorem show that on a common probability space we can build for each
m\geqslant 1
a Poisson counting process
\mathfrak{P}^{(m)}
and a Brownian motion
B
so that we have the
almost sure
convergence:

\displaystyle\frac{(\mathfrak{P}^{(m)}(tm)-tm)}{\sqrt{m}}\xrightarrow[m\to\infty]{a.s.}(B_{t}:t\geqslant 0)

(8.7)

for the uniform norm over every compact of
\mathbb{R}_{+}
. Recalling (
8.4
) those observations yield for
m=\lfloor n^{2/3}\rfloor

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathbb{S}^{[n,p_{n}]}_{\lfloor n^{2/3}t\rfloor}}{n^{1/3}}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant A}

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{\underset{\mathrm{for\ each\ }n}{=}}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathfrak{P}^{(m)}\left(n\left(1-\left(1-\frac{1}{n}-\frac{\lambda}{n^{4/3}}\right)^{\lfloor n^{2/3}t\rfloor}\right)\right)-\lfloor n^{2/3}t\rfloor}{n^{1/3}}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant A}

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{eq:dse}}{=}

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\mathfrak{P}^{(m)}\left(tm+\lambda t\sqrt{m}-\frac{t^{2}}{2}\sqrt{m}+o(\sqrt{m}))\right)-tm+o(\sqrt{m})}{\sqrt{m}+o(1)}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant A}

\displaystyle\underset{\eqref{eq:skorokhod}}{\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{a.s.}}

\displaystyle\left(B_{t}+\lambda t-\frac{t^{2}}{2}\right)_{0\leqslant t\leqslant A},

and this proves the proposition. ∎

8.4  Connectedness

As another application of our modification of the Erdős–Rényi random graph, let us give a short proof of the (very) sharp phase transition for connectedness in the fixed-size Erdős–Rényi which is mentioned in (
5.6
):

Theorem 8.6

(Critical window for connectedness
[
52
]
)
.

For
c\in\mathbb{R}
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(G\left(n,\frac{\log n+c}{n}\right)\mbox{ is connected}\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-c}}.

Proof.
Let
p\equiv p_{n}=\frac{\log n+c}{n}
. Connectedness of the core
G(n,p_{n})
is equivalent to the fact that
\mathrm{F^{stack}}(n,p_{n})
has only one non-trivial component (the others being isolated vertices of the stack), or equivalently that the Łukasiewicz walk
(\mathbb{S}^{(n,p_{n})})
starts with a (large) excursion and once it has reached level
-1
, it makes only jumps of
-1
forever. That is,
\mathbb{S}^{(n,p_{n})}_{n+1}=-1
and time
n+1
is the first hitting time of
-1
. In particular, in the notation (
8.2
) we must have
F_{n}(x^{(p_{n})}_{n+1})=n
or equivalently, that no uniform
U_{i}
for
1\leqslant i\leqslant n
falls after the point

x^{(p_{n})}_{n}=1-\left(1-\frac{\log n+c}{n}\right)^{n}\sim\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-c}}{n},\quad\mbox{ as }n\to\infty.

Computing this probability is routine and we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}U_{i}\leqslant x^{(p_{n})}_{n}\right)\sim\left(1-\frac{\mathrm{e}^{-c}}{n}\right)^{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{e}^{-c}}.

To finish the proof, one shows that as long as this event is realized, then the core is connected with high probability. In term of the Łukasiewicz walk this boils down to:

Lemma 8.7
.

For
p\equiv p_{n}=\frac{\log n+c}{n}
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\mathbb{S}^{(n,p_{n})}_{k}\geqslant 0:\forall 1\leqslant k\leqslant n\mid F_{n}(x^{(p_{n})}_{n})=n\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}1.

Proof of the lemma.

Notice that the event on which we are conditioning is of asymptotically positive probability, so it suffices to shows that
\mathbb{P}(\exists 1\leqslant k\leqslant n-1:\mathbb{S}^{(n,p_{n})}_{k}=0\mbox{ and }\mathbb{S}^{(n,p_{n})}_{n}=0)
tends to
0
. We perform a union bound over all such
k^{\prime}s
and compute

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\exists 1\leqslant k\leqslant n-1:\mathbb{S}^{(n,p_{n})}_{k}=0\ \&\ \mathbb{S}^{(n,p_{n})}_{n}=0)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\mathbb{P}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\#\{1\leqslant i\leqslant n:U_{i}\in I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup\dots\cup I_{k}\}=k\\
\#\{1\leqslant i\leqslant n:U_{i}\in I_{k+1}\cup I_{2}\cup\dots\cup I_{n}\}=n-k\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{n/2}\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(n,x^{(p_{n})}_{k})\leqslant k)+\sum_{k=n/2}^{n-1}\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(n,x^{(p_{n})}_{n}-x^{(p_{n})}_{k})\geqslant n-k).

For
\varepsilon_{n},\delta_{n}
tending to
0
such that
n\varepsilon_{n}\to\infty
as
n\to\infty
we use the bound

\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(n,\varepsilon_{n})\leqslant\delta_{n}\varepsilon_{n})\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{{}-\mathrm{c}\ n\varepsilon_{n}}\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(n,\varepsilon_{n})\geqslant n-\delta_{n}\varepsilon_{n})\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{c}\ n\varepsilon_{n}},

for some
c>0
. Since for
k\leqslant 10\frac{n}{\log n}
we have
k=o(nx^{(p_{n})}_{k})
we can apply the above bound and get for some
\mathrm{c}^{\prime}>0

\sum_{k=1}^{10n/\log n}\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(n,x^{(p_{n})}_{k})\leqslant k)\leqslant\sum_{k=1}^{10n/\log n}\exp(-\mathrm{c}\ nx^{(p_{n})}_{k})\leqslant\sum_{k=1}^{10n/\log n}\exp(-\mathrm{c^{\prime}}\ k\log n)=o(1).

The case when
10\frac{n}{\log n}\leqslant k\leqslant n/2
is even easier since we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(n,x^{(p_{n})}_{k})\leqslant k)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{Bin}(n,x^{(p_{n})}_{10\frac{n}{\log n}})\leqslant\frac{n}{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(n,1-2\mathrm{e}^{-10})\leqslant n/2)\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-{c}^{\prime\prime}n},

for some
c^{\prime\prime}>0
by a large deviation estimate since
1-2\mathrm{e}^{-10}>1/2
. Summing-up those estimates we deduce that
\sum_{k=1}^{n/2}\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(n,x^{(p_{n})}_{k})\leqslant k)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. A similar reasoning shows that
\sum_{k=n/2}^{n-1}\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Bin}(n,x^{(p_{n})}_{n}-x^{(p_{n})}_{k})\geqslant n-k)\to 0
as well, and we leave the verification as an exercise for the reader. ∎

Bibliographical notes.
The content of this chapter is adapted from the author’s paper
[
38
]
and from the master’s thesis of Damian Cid (promotion 2023-2024) who elegantly depoissonized the initial arguments. Various modifications of the Erdős–Rényi random graph with nicer probabilistic properties have been used in the literature, see e.g. the Poisson cloning model
[
74
]
.

Part
III
:
Random tree growth

In this part, we study several models of random growing trees where vertices are attached to the preceding structure according to some rule. The prototype is the random recursive tree process
(T_{n}:n\geqslant 0)
where
T_{n+1}
is obtained from
T_{n}
by attaching a new vertex labeled
n+1
onto a uniform vertex of
T_{n}
. We will study this process both from a static point of view (statistics of uniform random permutations), and from a dynamical process as
n
increases (Polya urns and continuous time embedding).

Figure 8.5:
A random recursive tree at stages
10,100
and
10000
.

IX
:
Random permutations

Many points of view on
n!

In this chapter, we study the law of the cycle decomposition of a random permutation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
chosen uniformly in the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
over
n
elements
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
. In particular, we shall establish Poisson statistics for the number of shorts cycles and the Poisson–Dirichlet limit for the large cycles.

9.1  Feller coupling

In 1945, Feller (the author of Lemma
3.4
) introduced a coupling between the cycle structure of a uniform permutation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
and the spacings between successes in a sequence of
n
independent Bernoulli variables of parameters
\frac{1}{n}
,
\frac{1}{n-1}
, …
\frac{1}{2},1
. This will be the main tool used in this chapter. The key idea of this representation is to explore a given permutation along its cycles ordered by their minimal element. A concept which is sometimes called the
Foata
1
1

1

Dominique Foata (1934–), French
correspondence
.

9.1.1   Foata correspondence

A permutation
\sigma_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
can obviously be described by a sequence
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})
representing the
n
values
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
, the most obvious way is to prescribe the permutation by its values
\sigma_{n}(1)=i_{1},\sigma_{n}(2)=i_{2},\dots,\sigma_{n}(n)=i_{n}
. Yet another way is to imagine that
(i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{n})
is the sequence of values we discover when exploring the cycles of
\sigma_{n}
ordered by their minimal values, see Figure
9.1
. Specifically, let us denote by

\displaystyle\big(a_{1}^{(1)},\dots,a_{k_{1}}^{(1)}\big)\big(a_{1}^{(2)},\dots,a_{k_{2}}^{(2)}\big)\cdots\big(a_{1}^{(\ell)},\dots,a_{k_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}\big),

(9.1)

the decomposition of
\sigma_{n}
into
\ell
cycles with disjoint supports of length
k_{1},\dots,k_{\ell}\geqslant 1
. We suppose that those cycles are ranked according to their minimal element, which is placed at the end of each cycle in this representation:

1=a_{k_{1}}^{(1)}=\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k_{1}}a_{i}^{(1)}<a_{k_{2}}^{(2)}=\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k_{2}}a_{i}^{(2)}<\cdots<a_{k_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}=\min_{1\leqslant i\leqslant k_{\ell}}a_{i}^{(\ell)}.

Then, the Foata encoding of
\sigma_{n}
is the permutation we obtain by reading the numbers in (
9.1
) from left to right, namely

\mathrm{Foata}(\sigma_{n})=\big(a_{1}^{(1)},\dots,a_{k_{1}}^{(1)},a_{1}^{(2)},\dots,a_{k_{2}}^{(2)},\cdots,a_{1}^{(\ell)},\dots,a_{k_{\ell}}^{(\ell)}\big).

It is then clear that
\mathrm{Foata}:\mathfrak{S}_{n}\to\mathfrak{S}_{n}
is a bijection and furthermore that the number of cycles of
\sigma_{n}
is equal to the number of minimal records of
\mathrm{Foata}(\sigma_{n})
, i.e. the values
k
such that
\mathrm{Foata}(\sigma_{n})_{k}=\min\{\mathrm{Foata}(\sigma_{n})_{i}:i\geqslant k\}
and that the length of the cycles correspond to the spacing between those records.

Figure 9.1:
Foata correspondence: on the left a description of a permutation via its images, on the right the description of a permutation by exploration of its cycles ranked in increasing order of their minimal element. This bijection transforms the number of cycles into the number of minimal records (in red on the left).

Exercise 9.1

(Law of a typical cycle)
.

Show using the Foata correspondence that the size of the cycle containing
1
in a uniform permutation is uniform on
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
.

9.1.2   Feller coupling

Keeping in mind the Foata encoding of a permutation, we now present the famous result of Feller. We consider
n
independent Bernoulli variables
\mathrm{Ber}(\frac{1}{k})
of success parameters

\frac{1}{n};\quad\frac{1}{n-1};\quad\dots\quad\frac{1}{2};\quad\frac{1}{1}.

Denote by
n\geqslant I_{1}>\dots>I_{\ell}=1
the indices (the reciprocal of the parameter) of the variables equal to
1
and consider the
\ell
spacings
\mathcal{S}_{n}=((n+1)-I_{1},I_{1}-I_{2},\dots,I_{\ell-1}-I_{\ell})
between the points
n+1>I_{1}>\dots>I_{\ell}
. The sum of those spacings is equal to
n
.

Figure 9.2:
Constructing the law of the length of the cycles (in orange above) in a random permutation via the spacings in Bernoulli trials with parameters
1/(n-i)
for
i\in\{0,1,2,\dots,n-1\}
. The red dots correspond to successes. Notice that we start with a space of length
1
just before the first trial of parameter
1/n
.

Theorem 9.1

(Feller)
.

The spacings
\mathcal{S}_{n}
between successes of the above Bernoulli variables have the same law as the cycle lengths of a uniform permutation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
when ordered as in the Foata construction (
9.1
).

Proof.
Let us explore the cycle structure of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
step by step. Consider first the cycle containing
1
in
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
. Then,
1
is a fixed point with probability
1/n
–this corresponds to success of
\mathrm{Ber}(1/n)
– otherwise, it is sent via
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
to a value
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}(1)
uniformly distributed over
\{2,3,\dots,n\}
. Conditionally on
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}(1)\neq 1
, a simple calculation shows that we have
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}(1))=1
with probability
\frac{1}{n-1}
–this corresponds to the success of
\mathrm{Ber}(1/(n-1))
– or it is sent to a value
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{2}(1)\notin\{1,\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}(1)\}
. Iteratively, if after
k\geqslant 2
iterations, conditionally on
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{j}(1)\neq 1
for all
1\leqslant j\leqslant k-1
, we have
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{k}_{n}(1)=1
with probability
\frac{1}{n-k}
–corresponding to the success of
\mathrm{Ber}(1/(n-k))
– otherwise the cycle continues. Hence, the length of the cycle containing
1
indeed has the same law as the first spacing
(n+1)-I_{1}
in the Bernoulli trials. Once the cycle of
1
of length
k_{1}
, has been entirely explored, if
k_{1}<n
we can relabel the remaining values in increasing order by
\{1,2,\dots,n-k_{1}\}
and it is easy to see that the permutation
\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}_{n-k_{1}}
induced by
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
on these values, is, conditionally on the exploration of the first cycle, uniform over
\mathfrak{S}_{n-k_{1}}
so that we can iterate the procedure. ∎

A direct consequence of the above theorem is that the law of the length of the cycle containing the point
i_{0}\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
in the random permutation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
is a uniform variable over
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
(see Exercise
9.1
). Also, the number of cycles
\mathcal{C}_{n}
of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
can be expressed as
\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}B_{k}
where
B_{k}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(1/k)
are independent, which is easily handled:

Proposition 9.2

(Law of the number of cycles in a uniform permutation)
.

For any
z\in\mathbb{C}
we have

\mathbb{E}[z^{\mathcal{C}_{n}}]=\prod_{j=0}^{n-1}\frac{z+j}{j+1}.

As a result, its expectation and variance satisfy
\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{C}_{n}]=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{1}{k}\sim\log n
and
\mathrm{Var}(\mathcal{C}_{n})\sim\log n
as
n\to\infty
, and we have a central limit theorem

\frac{\mathcal{C}_{n}-\log n}{\sqrt{\log n}}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(d)}\mathcal{N}(0,1).

Proof.
The formula for the generating function is easily proven using the equality in law
\mathcal{C}_{n}=\sum_{1\leqslant k\leqslant n}\mathrm{Ber}(1/k)
where the Bernoulli variables are independent as in Theorem
9.1
. Taking expectation yields the harmonic sum, while taking variance yields to the sum of the variances which is
\sum_{k=1}^{n}(\tfrac{1}{k}-\tfrac{1}{k^{2}})\sim\log n
. The central limit theorem can be proved by evaluating the Fourier transform and using Lévy’s theorem (but we shall see another estimation-free route in Proposition
11.13
). ∎

Exercise 9.2
.

Show that
\mathbb{E}[2^{\mathcal{C}_{n}}]=n+1
. Do you have a combinatorial interpretation?

9.2  Large cycles and Poisson–Dirichlet distribution

In this section, we use Theorem
9.1
to compute the law of the large cycles of a uniform permutation in the scaling limit. Perhaps surprisingly, the law of the random partition of
1
we obtain pops-up in other contexts such as in the factorization of large random integers.

9.2.1   Stick breaking construction

Let
U_{1},U_{2},\dots
be a sequence of independent identically distributed uniform variables on
[0,1]
. We use these variables to perform a “stick breaking” of the interval
[0,1]
by setting

X_{1}=(1-U_{1}),\quad X_{2}=U_{1}(1-U_{2}),\quad X_{3}=U_{1}U_{2}(1-U_{3})\dots.

By the law of large numbers we have

\prod_{i\geqslant 1}^{n}U_{i}=\exp\Big(\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\log U_{i}}_{=-n+o_{a.s.}(n)}\Big)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{a.s.}0.

and in particular we have
\sum X_{i}=1
with probability one.

9.1
Poisson–Dirichlet.

The Poisson–Dirichlet distribution is the law of the lengths
X_{1},X_{2},\dots
in the above stick-breaking construction.

Ranked version.

Although the variables
X_{i}
are stochastically decreasing in
i
, the sequence
(X_{i}:i\geqslant 1)
is not decreasing in general. Sometimes the law of
(X_{i}:i\geqslant 1)
is called the GEM (Griffiths, Engen, McCloskey) law and the Poisson–Dirichlet is its version
(X_{i}^{\downarrow}:i\geqslant 1)
ranked in decreasing order. In these notes, we shall use the name Poisson–Dirichlet for both laws, the context making clear what we mean. The ranked version may seem more appropriate (at least to state convergence results), but actually the initial version is much more convenient from a probabilistic point of view.

Figure 9.3:
Five simulations of the Poisson–Dirichlet (unranked) partition.

A corollary of Theorem
9.1
is the following:

Theorem 9.3

(Poisson–Dirichlet as limit of cycle length)
.

For
n\geqslant 0
we denote by
K_{1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}),K_{2}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}),\dots
the cycle lengths appearing in the Foata encoding of a uniform permutation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
as in (
9.1
). Then we have the following convergence in distribution

\displaystyle\left(\frac{K_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n})}{n}:i\geqslant 1\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(d)}(X_{i}:i\geqslant 1),

(9.2)

for the
\ell_{1}
-distance on the space of sequences
\ell_{1}^{(1)}=\{(x_{i})_{i\geqslant 1}:x_{i}>0\mbox{ and }\sum_{i}x_{i}=1\}
. Consequently, if
K_{1}^{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n})\geqslant K_{2}^{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n})\geqslant\cdots
are the cycle lengths of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
ranked in non-increasing order, then we have

\displaystyle\left(\frac{K_{i}^{\downarrow}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n})}{n}:i\geqslant 1\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(d)}(X^{\downarrow}_{i}:i\geqslant 1),

(9.3)

Proof.
In Feller’s coupling, it is straightforward to compute the law of the first spacing which is
N=(n+1)-\sup\{k\leqslant n:\mathrm{Ber}(1/k)=1\}
. As already remarked (see Exercise
9.1
), this law is uniform over
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and conditionally on it, the remaining spacings have the law of
\mathcal{S}_{n-N}
. It follows that if
\mathcal{S}_{n}(1),\mathcal{S}_{n}(2)
are the ordered spacings (when read from the parameter
1/n
down to
1
) satisfy
n^{-1}\cdot\mathcal{S}_{n}(1)\to U_{1}
and recursively

\left(\frac{\mathcal{S}_{n}(i)}{n}:i\geqslant 1\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(d)}(X_{i}:i\geqslant 1),

in terms of finite-dimensional convergence. Actually, since we know that
n^{-1}\mathcal{S}_{n}
and
(X_{i}:i\geqslant 1)
belong to
\ell_{1}^{(1)}
(they sum-up to
1
) the finite dimensional convergence implies the
\ell_{1}
convergence in law. The last convergence follows by the mapping theorem since reordering of a sequence is a continuous operation on
\ell_{1}^{(1)}
. ∎

Remark 9.1

(Size-biasing and split merge dynamic)
.

Let us give two distributional properties of the ranked Poisson–Dirichlet partition
(X_{i}^{\downarrow}:i\geqslant 1)
which are not easy to prove in the continuous setting, but whose analogs in the discrete setting are obvious.

Let us imagine
(X_{i}^{\downarrow}:i\geqslant 1)
as a stick breaking of the interval
[0,1]
into countably many intervals, and let
V\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,1]
be a uniform point chosen independently of this stick breaking. Then the size of the interval containing the point
V
(there is almost surely no tie) is uniformly distribution on
[0,1]
. This can be shown by considering the cycle length of a uniform point
V_{n}\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
in
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
.

Similarly, there is a natural dynamic on random permutations
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
which preserves the uniform distribution: just compose (to the left or to the right)
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
by a transposition
\tau_{i,j}
where
i,j\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
are i.i.d. uniform. In terms of the cycle structure, this gives rise to a split-merge transform. In the continuous setup, this boils down to sampling
V,V^{\prime}
independently of the stick breaking
(X_{i}^{\downarrow}:i\geqslant 1)
: if the two points fall into two distinct intervals, then those two pieces are merged. Otherwise, the interval containing both
V
and
V^{\prime}
is split into two intervals uniformly. The Poisson–Dirichlet law is an invariant measure for this dynamic (and is in fact the only one, see
[
45
]
).

Perhaps surprisingly, the Poisson–Dirichlet law appears in many other “logarithmic combinatorial structures” such as factorization of random polynomials over finite fields or prime factorization of large random integers:

Theorem 9.4

(Billingsley)
.

Let
N\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
be a uniform integer less than or equal to
n
and let
p^{\downarrow}_{1}(N)\geqslant p^{\downarrow}_{2}(N)\geqslant\dots
its prime factors (with possible repetition). Then we have

\left(\frac{\log p^{\downarrow}_{i}(N)}{\log n};i\geqslant 1\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(d)}(X_{i}^{\downarrow}:i\geqslant 1).

We refer to
[
14
]
for details.

9.2.2   Dickman function and prisoners

In this section, we present Dickman
2
2

2

Karl Dickman (1861–1947), Swedish. He was actuary and published only one article in mathematics
[
46
]
introducing this function when he was around
70
.
function which is essentially the tail distribution
X_{1}^{\downarrow}
, the scaling limit of the longest cycle in a random permutation. This function pops up in various places in analytic number theory and has intriguing properties.

Proposition 9.5

(Dickman function)
.

Consider
(X_{i}^{\downarrow}:i\geqslant 1)
the ranked version of a Poisson–Dirichlet distribution. Then for
x\geqslant 0
we have

\mathbb{P}(X_{1}^{\downarrow}\leqslant x)=\rho(1/x),

where
x\mapsto\rho(x)
is Dickman’s function defined by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{lc}\rho(x)=1&\mbox{ for }0\leqslant x\leqslant 1\\
x\rho^{\prime}(x)=-\rho(x-1)&\mbox{ for }x\geqslant 1.\end{array}\right.

Figure 9.4
:
Dickman’s function

Proof.
We use the notation
\mathbb{P}(X_{1}^{\downarrow}\leqslant x)=\rho(1/x)
extended to
\rho(u)=1
for
u\in[0,1]
. In the unranked version
(X_{i}:i\geqslant 1)
of the Poisson–Dirichlet partition we can write after conditioning on the first uniform variable
U_{1}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X_{1}^{\downarrow}\leqslant x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\{X_{1}\leqslant x\}\cap\{X_{2},\dots,X_{n},\dots\leqslant x\})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{1-U_{1}\leqslant x}\mathbb{P}\left(\tilde{X}_{1}^{\downarrow}\leqslant\frac{x}{U_{1}}\right)\right],

which give the following integral equation

\displaystyle\rho(1/x)=\mathbb{P}(X_{1}^{\downarrow}\leqslant x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}\mathrm{d}u\,\mathbb{P}(X_{1}^{\downarrow}\leqslant\frac{x}{1-u})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{x}\mathrm{d}u\,\rho\left(\frac{1-u}{x}\right)

\displaystyle\rho(y)

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}v=u/x\\
y=1/x\end{subarray}}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{y}\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}v\,\rho(y-v).

Differentiating the equality
y\rho(y)=\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}v\,\rho(y-v)
with respect to
y
, we recover the delayed differential equation of the proposition. ∎

Related to the Dickman function, let us state a famous riddle:

The director of a prison offers 100 death row prisoners, who are numbered from 1 to 100, a last chance. A room contains a cupboard with 100 drawers. The director randomly puts one prisoner’s number in each closed drawer. The prisoners enter the room, one after another. Each prisoner may open and look into 50 drawers in any order. The drawers are closed again afterwards. If, during this search, every prisoner finds their number in one of the drawers, all prisoners are pardoned. If even one prisoner does not find their number, all prisoners die. Before the first prisoner enters the room, the prisoners may discuss strategy Ñ but may not communicate once the first prisoner enters to look in the drawers. What is the prisoners’ best strategy?

Opening
50
drawers at random (independently for each prisoner) is a hopeless strategy since the probability that they all manage to find their numbers is
(1/2)^{100}\approx 0
. However, they can correlate their searchs if the
i
th prisoner starts with
i
th drawer, looks at the discovered label and successively follows the cycle of the underlying permutation of the labels. The probability of success is the probability that no cycle of the permutation of the labels has a length larger than
50
which is approximately
\mathbb{P}(X_{1}^{\downarrow}\leqslant 1/2)=1-\log 2\approx 30\%
.

Formulas without words.

Euler’s constant

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{\lfloor x\rfloor}-\frac{1}{x}\right)\mathrm{d}x=\log\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}x\,\rho(x).

Golomb-Dickman constant

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{exp}\left(\int_{0}^{x}\frac{\mathrm{d}t}{\ln t}\right)=\mathbb{E}[X_{1}^{\downarrow}]=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\frac{\rho(t)}{(t+1)^{2}}.

\displaystyle\sum_{k\geqslant 1}\prod_{j=1}^{k}U_{j}

\displaystyle\overset{(d)}{=}

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-\gamma}\rho(x)\mathrm{d}x.

where
U_{i}
are i.i.d. uniforms on
[0,1]

9.3  Poisson count for short cycles

In the previous section, we saw that the Poisson–Dirichlet law is the limit law of the large cycles in a random uniform permutation. However, the information about the small cycles is lost in this limit and we will see below that they are ruled by the Poisson paradigm already encountered in Section
5.3.1
.

9.3.1   An abstract limit from the Feller coupling

Recall the setup of Theorem
9.1
, let
(B_{k}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(1/k):k\geqslant 1)
be independent Bernoulli variables of parameter
1/k
and denote by
1=\tilde{I}_{1}<\tilde{I}_{2}<\cdots
the indices of the variables equal to
1
(beware we see those variables as indexed “in the other direction” compared to the previous section). In a sense, the spacings between
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}:=(\tilde{I}_{k}:k\geqslant 1)
could be seen as the cycle structure of an “infinite permutation”. Down to earth, we have

\forall A\geqslant 1,\quad\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(\tilde{I}_{k+1}-\tilde{I}_{k}=A)=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}(\tilde{I}_{k}=i,\tilde{I}_{k+1}=i+A)\leqslant\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{i(i+A)}<\infty,

so that the Borel–Cantelli lemma shows that
\tilde{I}_{k+1}-\tilde{I}_{k}\to\infty
almost surely as
k\to\infty
. In particular, we can define the increasing rearrangement of the spacings between consecutive points in
(\tilde{I}_{k}:k\geqslant 1)
and their count

\mathcal{N}_{i}:=\#\{k\geqslant 1:\tilde{I}_{k+1}-\tilde{I}_{k}=i\}<\infty.

Below we write
N_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n})
for the
number of cycles
of length
i
in the decomposition of the random uniform permutation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
into product of cycles with disjoint supports. Given Theorem
9.1
, it is rather straightforward to show that
(N_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}):i\geqslant 1)
converge in law as
n\to\infty
:

Proposition 9.6
.

We have the convergence in law (in the sense of finite-dimensional marginals)

\displaystyle(N_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}):i\geqslant 1)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(d)}(\mathcal{N}_{i}:i\geqslant 1).

(9.4)

Proof.
Feller’s coupling (Theorem
9.1
) provides a way to couple uniform permutations
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{fel}}:n\geqslant 1)
on a common probability space so that
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{fel}}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
in law and such that the cycle structure of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{fel}}
coincides with the spacings between the points
1=\tilde{I}_{1}<\tilde{I}_{2}<\cdots<\tilde{I}_{\ell_{n}}<(n+1)
where
\tilde{I}_{\ell_{n}}
is the last index strictly before
(n+1)
. In this coupling we
nearly
have the almost sure convergence
N_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{fel}})\to\mathcal{N}_{i}
as
n\to\infty
. The reason that the coupling falls short of proving this point-wise convergence is that if
(n+1)
is large and located precisely
i_{0}
unit after a point of
\mathcal{S}_{\infty}
(with no other point in-between) then we have
N_{i_{0}}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{fel}})=\mathcal{N}_{i_{0}}+1
. However, for any positive function
f
bounded by
C>0
and any
k_{0}\geqslant 1
we have

\displaystyle\Big|\mathbb{E}[f(N_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{fel}}):1\leqslant i\leqslant k_{0})]-\mathbb{E}[f(\mathcal{N}_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant k_{0})]\Big|

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle C\Big(\mathbb{P}(S_{\infty}\cap\{n-k_{0},\dots,n-1,n\}\neq\varnothing)+\mathbb{P}(\exists\tilde{I}_{\ell}\geqslant n\mbox{ with }\tilde{I}_{\ell+1}-\tilde{I}_{\ell}\leqslant k_{0})\Big)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}

\displaystyle 0.

The desired convergence in law follows. ∎

We will see in Theorem
9.9
below that the law of
(\mathcal{N}_{i}:i\geqslant 1)
is actually super simple! A simple way to see this, is to take a small detour using Cauchy’s formula and to randomize the permutation’s length. This operation, usually called Poissonnization, will be made crystal clear in Chapter
11
.

9.3.2   Cauchy’s formula and interpretation

The starting point is a famous formula due to Cauchy giving the exact law of the cycle-counting function. With the notation above we have:

Proposition 9.7

(Cauchy)
.

For any
c_{1},c_{2},\dots,c_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geqslant 0}
so that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}ic_{i}=n
we have

\mathbb{P}(N_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n})=c_{i},\forall 1\leqslant i\leqslant n)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{(1/i)^{c_{i}}}{(c_{i})!}.

Proof.
Once the cycle structure
(c_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant n)
of the permutation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
has been fixed (with the obvious constraint), the number of possible candidates is obtained by:

•

distributing the
n
numbers
1,2,\dots,n
into the
\sum c_{i}
boxes of sizes
1,2,\dots,i,\dots,n
: since the
c_{i}
boxes of size
i
are indistinguishable, there are

\left(\frac{n!}{\cdots\underbrace{i!\cdot i!}_{c_{i}\mathrm{\ times}}\cdots}\right)\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{c_{i}!}\quad\mbox{ such choices}.

•

then constructing an
i
-cycle with the numbers in each box of size
i
: there are
(i-1)!
possibilities each.

We deduce that the probability in the proposition is given by

\frac{1}{n!}\cdot\frac{n!}{\prod_{i=1}^{n}(i!)^{c_{i}}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{c_{i}!}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\big((i-1)!\big)^{c_{i}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{(1/i)^{c_{i}}}{(c_{i})!}.

∎

Let us put our probabilist’s glasses on and interpret the previous formula as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(N_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n})=c_{i},\forall 1\leqslant i\leqslant n)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{(1/i)^{c_{i}}}{(c_{i})!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{1+\frac{1}{2}+\cdots+\frac{1}{n}}\cdot\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathrm{e}^{-1/i}\frac{(1/i)^{c_{i}}}{(c_{i})!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{\mathrm{h}_{n}}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{P}(Z_{i}=c_{i}),

where
(Z_{i}:i\geqslant 1)
are independent Poisson random variables with mean
1/i
, and where
\mathrm{h}_{n}
is the
n
th harmonic sum. In other words, the vector
(N_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}):1\leqslant i\leqslant n)
has the same law as
(Z_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant n)
conditioned on the event
\{\sum_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n}iZ_{i}=n\}
. This observation, due to Kolchin, can actually be pushed a little further as remarked by Lloyd & Shepp. Denote by
\sigma_{0}
the permutation with
0
cycles so that
N_{i}(\sigma_{0})=0
for all
i\geqslant 1
. For
x\in(0,1)
, for any sequence
(c_{i}:i\geqslant 1)
of integers which is eventually the null sequence, if we denote by
N=\sum ic_{i}
then we have

\displaystyle(1-x)\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}x^{n}\mathbb{P}\big(N_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n})=c_{i}:\forall 1\leqslant i\leqslant n\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1-x)x^{N}\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{(1/i)^{c_{i}}}{(c_{i})!}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\underbrace{(1-x)\mathrm{e}^{x+\frac{x^{2}}{2}+\cdots+\frac{x^{n}}{n}+\cdots}}_{1}\prod_{i=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-x^{i}/i}\frac{(x^{i}/i)^{c_{i}}}{(c_{i})!}

This means:

Lemma 9.8
.

If
\mathbf{n}_{x}\in\{0,1,2,\dots\}
is a geometric random variable with mean
\frac{x}{1-x}
and if, conditionally on
\mathbf{n}_{x}
, we let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathbf{n}_{x}}
be a uniform permutation on
\mathfrak{S}_{\mathbf{n}_{x}}
, then the cycle counts
(N_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathbf{n}_{x}}):i\geqslant 1)
has the same law as independent Poisson random variables with means
\frac{x^{i}}{i}
for
i\geqslant 1
.

We will see in Chapter
11
that the above lemma follows from combining the construction of the random recursive tree from a Yule process in continuous time and the Chinese restaurant process (sic!).

Exercise 9.3

(Random
\zeta
-number)
.

For
s>1,
consider
\mathbf{N}_{s}\in\{1,2,\dots\}
a random number sampled according to

\mathbb{P}(\mathbf{N}_{s}=n)=\frac{1}{\zeta(s)}n^{-s}.

Show that the
p
-valuations
(\nu_{p}(\mathbf{N}_{s}):p\in\mathcal{P})
are independent geometric random variables with success parameters
(1/p)^{s}
for all prime numbers
p\in\mathcal{P}
.

9.3.3   Poisson limit

We are now armed to prove the following:

Theorem 9.9

(Goncharov, Kolchin)
.

Recall that
N_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n})
is the number of cycles of length
i\geqslant 1
in the decomposition of the uniform permutation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
into product of cycles with disjoint supports. Then we have the following convergence in law for the finite-dimensional marginals

\big(N_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}):i\geqslant 1\big)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(d)}\big(\mathrm{Poi}(1/i):i\geqslant 1\big),

where the Poisson random variables on the right-hand side are independent and of mean
1/i
for
i\geqslant 1
.

Remark 9.2

(Derangements)
.

We recover the famous asymptotic of the number of derangements (permutations without fixed points) since the last theorem implies in particular that as
n\to\infty
we have

\frac{\#\{{\sigma_{n}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}:{\sigma}_{n}\mbox{ has no fixed points}\}}{n!}=\mathbb{P}(N_{1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n})=0)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{}\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Poi}(1)=0)=\mathrm{e}^{-1}.

In fact, the inclusion-exclusion principle shows that we have the explicit series representation
\sum_{k=0}^{n}(-1)^{k}\frac{n!}{k!}
for the number of derangements of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
.

Proof.
We already know from (
9.4
) that
(N_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}):i\geqslant 1)
converges in law towards some limiting vector
(\mathcal{N}_{i}:i\geqslant 1)
as
n\to\infty
. On the other hand, if we let
x\to 1
in Lemma
9.8
we deduce that
\mathbf{n}_{x}\to\infty
in probability. Since conditionally on
\mathbf{n}_{x}
the permutation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathbf{n}_{x}}
is uniform, we deduce that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}(N_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{\mathbf{n}_{x}}):i\geqslant 1)&\xrightarrow[x\to 1]{(d)}&(\mathcal{N}_{i}:i\geqslant 1)\\
\rotatebox{90.0}{=}\quad\mbox{in law}&&\quad\rotatebox{90.0}{=}\quad\mbox{in law}\\
\big(\mathrm{Poi}(x^{i}/i):i\geqslant 1\big)&\xrightarrow[x\to 1]{(d)}&\big(\mathrm{Poi}(1/i):i\geqslant 1\big).\end{array}

where all the Poisson variables are independent. ∎

Remark 9.3

(Direct calculation)
.

It can be seen directly that the variables in (
9.4
) are independent Poisson variables with mean
1/i
without referring to random permutations. In fact, once the limit has been re-interpreted as the spacings between records of i.i.d. uniforms on
[0,1]
, it is a consequence of a more general theorem due to Ignatov on the Poissonnian structure of records values of a Markov process. We refer the interested reader to
[
93
]
and
[
100
]
for details.

Bibliographical notes.

There are many references on the popular subject of random permutation, see e.g. the Saint-Flour lectures of Pitman
[
97
]
in particular Section 3.1 or the Bible in combinatorics
[
59
]
. Various sets of lecture notes are also available on the web such as
[
58
,
60
]
and more recent results about “logarithmic combinatorial structures” can be found in
[
14
]
. Feller’s coupling is proved in
[
56
]
, and the Poisson counting limit is due to Goncharov and Kolchin, but our proof based on Lemma
9.8
is inspired from Lloyd and Shepp
[
104
]
. For more about appearance of Dickman’s function in probabilistic and analytic number theory, see
[
110
]
and
[
30
]
. We also refer to
[
29
]
for other applications of the randomization technique to random partitions.

Hints for Exercises.

Exercise
9.1
: The size of the cycle containing
1
in
\sigma_{n}
is equal to the value of the pre-image of
1
in
\mathrm{Foata}(\sigma_{n})
.

Exercise
9.2
: A random permutation sampled according to
2^{\#\mathrm{number\ of\ cycles}}
appears in Toth’s representation of the quantum Heisenberg ferromagnet on the complete graph (sic!), see
[
111
]
.

Exercise
9.3
: Re-interpret the Eulerian product formula
\displaystyle\prod_{p\in\mathcal{P}}\left(\frac{1}{1-\frac{1}{p^{s}}}\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{s}}.

X
:
Random recursive tree

LÕarbre, cÕest cette puissance qui lentement épouse le ciel.

A. de Saint-Exupéry

In this chapter we study the following random tree growth model:

10.1
RRT.

The
random recursive tree
(RRT) is the Markov chain with values in the set of all unoriented labeled trees such that
T_{0}=

\raisebox{-.9pt} {0}⃝
and so that for
n\geqslant 1
, conditionally on
T_{n-1}
, the labeled tree
T_{n}
is obtained by attaching the new vertex
\raisebox{-.6pt} {$n$}⃝
onto a uniform vertex of
T_{n-1}
.

Figure 10.1:
A simulation of
T_{10000}
where the root vertex
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}⃝
is placed at the top. Clearly, the random recursive tree seems “short and fat”.

Obviously there are
n!
possible values for
T_{n}
: these are all
increasing labeled trees with
n+1
vertices
i.e. unoriented trees labeled from
0
up to
n
and so that the labels along each branch starting from
\raisebox{-.9pt} {0}⃝
are increasing. For each
n\geqslant 0
, the RRT
T_{n}
is a uniform random variable over this set. We shall start with a link between this model of random tree and random permutations of the symmetric group
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
over
n
elements.

10.1  Chinese Restaurant process

Since there are
n!
equiprobable values for
T_{n}
, the RRT stopped at time
n
can be seen as an encoding of a uniform permutation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
of
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
. Moreover, it is possible to couple these encodings in a particularly nice way so that it is coherent for all
n\geqslant 1
simultaneously: this is the so-called
Chinese restaurant process
(CRP). This coupling is different from Feller’s coupling seen in the previous chapter.

10.1.1   Coupling CRP-RRT

Let
\sigma_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
be a permutation over
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
. If
n\geqslant 2
, we can canonically associate with
\sigma_{n}
a permutation
[\sigma_{n}]_{n-1}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n-1}
as follows: it is the permutation defined for
k\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
by

\left\{\begin{array}[]{cl}[\sigma_{n}]_{n-1}(k)=\sigma_{n}(k)&\mbox{ if }\sigma_{n}(k)\neq n,\\
[\sigma_{n}]_{n-1}(k)=\sigma_{n}(n)&\mbox{ if }\sigma_{n}(k)=n.\\
\end{array}\right.

The effect of removing the value
n
from
\sigma_{n}
is better understood on the cycle decomposition: the permutation
[\sigma_{n}]_{n-1}
is obtained by removing the value
n
in the cycle of
\sigma_{n}
which contains it. By extending the restriction step by step we can define
[\sigma_{n}]_{k}
for all
k\leqslant n
and it is easy to see that if
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
is uniform over
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
then
[\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}]_{k}
is also uniformly distributed over
\mathfrak{S}_{k}
.

Actually, it is easy to reverse the procedure and construct a sequence of random permutations
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{cr}}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}:n\geqslant 1)
as a Markov chain. Specifically, let
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{1}=(1)
and for
n\geqslant 2
, conditionally on
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n-1}^{\mathrm{cr}}
, the permutation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{cr}}
is obtained with probability
1/n
by just declaring
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{cr}}(n)=n
and with probability
1-\frac{1}{n}
by picking a uniform integer
k\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
and declaring that

\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{n}(k)=n\quad\mbox{and }\quad\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{n}(n)=\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{n-1}(k),

the others values being unchanged between
\sigma_{n}^{{\mathrm{cr}}}
and
\sigma_{n-1}^{{\mathrm{cr}}}
.
With the above notation we have
[\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{n}]_{k}=\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{k}^{\mathrm{cr}}
and this Markov chain produces a coupling of permutations uniformly distributed over
\mathfrak{S}_{n}
for each
n
.

The evolution of the cycle structure of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{n}
in the previous Markov chain is described by the following mechanism called the
Chinese restaurant process
1
1

1

Jim Pitman (1949–), Australian
: In this process, customers
1,2,3\dots
arrive sequentially in an imaginary (Chinese) restaurant. At step
n=1
, the customer
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$1$}⃝
arrives and sits at a new table. Inductively at step
n\geqslant 2
, the customer
\raisebox{-.6pt} {$n$}⃝
sits at the right of any of
n-1
previous customers with probability
\tfrac{1}{n}
or creates a new table with probability
\tfrac{1}{n}
. It should be clear from the above construction that the tables in the Chinese restaurant process describe the cycle structure of the growing sequence of permutations
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{\mathrm{cr}}_{n}:n\geqslant 1)
.

The Chinese restaurant process is canonically coupled with the RRT
(T_{n}:n\geqslant 0)
by declaring that the new customer
n
corresponds to the vertex
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$n$}⃝
and it attaches in
T_{n}
to the vertex corresponding to the customer on its left, or to the vertex
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}⃝
if this customer creates a new table. See Figure
10.2
. Thanks to this coupling, we deduce in particular that the degree of
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}⃝
in
T_{n}
is equal to the number of cycles in the cycle decomposition of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{cr}}
.

Figure 10.2:
Illustration of the coupling between growing permutations
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{cr}}:n\geqslant 1)
on the left, the Chinese restaurant process in the middle and the random recursive tree
(T_{n}:n\geqslant 1)
starting with initial state
0
on the right.

10.1.2   Pólya urn and almost sure convergence towards Poisson–Dirichlet

The Chinese restaurant coupling
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{cr}}:n\geqslant 1)
is a different coupling compared to Feller’s coupling
(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{fel}}:n\geqslant 1)
used in the proof of Proposition
9.6
. Roughly speaking, in the Chinese restaurant coupling, the structure of large cycles converges almost surely (see below), whereas in Feller’s coupling the structure of small cycles (nearly) converges almost surely. Recalling Theorem
9.3
, we have here:

Theorem 10.1

(Almost sure convergence of the Chinese restaurant process)
.

Let
(K_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{cr}}):i\geqslant 1)
be the cycle lengths of
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{cr}}
in the Foata encoding (
9.1
) i.e. the table sizes ranked by order of creation in the Chinese restaurant process. If
(X_{i}:i\geqslant 1)
is the (unranked) Poisson–Dirichlet random partition of
[0,1]
(see Definition
9.1
) then we have the following convergence in law in
\ell_{1}^{(1)}

\left(\frac{K_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{cr}})}{n}:i\geqslant 1\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{a.s.}(X_{i}:i\geqslant 1).

To prove the theorem let us first focus on the behavior of the process

(R_{n},B_{n}):=\left(K_{1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{cr}}),1+\sum_{i\geqslant 2}K_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{cr}})\right),

for
n\geqslant 1
.
It is clear from the definition of the Chinese restaurant process that this is a Markov chain starting from
R_{1}=1,B_{1}=1
and with transition probabilities given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(R_{n+1}=R_{n}+1\mid R_{n},B_{n})=\frac{R_{n}}{R_{n}+B_{n}},\quad\mathbb{P}(B_{n+1}=B_{n}+1\mid R_{n},B_{n})=\frac{B_{n}}{R_{n}+B_{n}}.

(10.1)

We recognize here the (law of the) famous
Pólya
2
2

2

George (György) Pólya (1887-1985), Hungarian
urn
, which is the stochastic system informally described as follows: initially at time
n=1
an urn contains one red ball and one blue ball. At each step, a ball is drawn from the urn uniformly at random and is replaced in the urn
together
with a new ball of the same color (re-inforcement). Then the number
(R_{n},B_{n})
of red and blue balls at step
n
is clearly a Markov chain with transitions (
10.1
).

Figure 10.3:
Mecanism of the standard Polya urn: a ball is drawn uniformly at random and is replaced together with a ball of the same color (reinforcement). On the right, the transitions for the number of balls of each color over the first steps of the process.

Proposition 10.2

(Convergence of proportions)
.

In the standard Polya urn started with 1 ball of each color, the proportion of red balls converges towards a uniform random variable on
[0,1]
.

Proof.
It is straightforward to check that
R_{n}/(R_{n}+B_{n})
is a bounded martingale (for the canonical filtration) which thus converges almost surely towards a limiting proportion
U\in[0,1]
. An easy induction on
n\geqslant 1
shows that
R_{n}
is uniformly distributed over
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and so

\frac{R_{n}}{R_{n}+B_{n}}=\frac{R_{n}}{n+1}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{a.s.}U\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,1].

In the next chapter, we will see another proof of this result based on continuous time techniques. ∎

Exercise 10.1

(Asymmetric starting configuration)
.

Compute the law of the limiting proportion of red balls when the Polya urn starts from
R_{0}=a
and
B_{0}=N_{0}-a
balls.

Proof of Theorem
10.1
.
The above discussion, together with Proposition
10.2
translated in the framework of the theorem, shows the almost sure convergence
n^{-1}\cdot K_{1}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{cr}})\to U_{1}
where
U_{1}
is uniform over
[0,1]
. However, it is easy to see that conditionally on the values

u_{k}=\inf\left\{t\geqslant 0:1+\sum_{i\geqslant 2}K_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{t}^{\mathrm{cr}})=k\right\},

the restricted process
(K_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{u_{k}}^{\mathrm{cr}}):i\geqslant 2)_{k\geqslant 1}
has the law of a Chinese restaurant process (thus independent of
U_{1}
). By successive applications of the above reasoning we deduce that

\frac{K_{i}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n})}{n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{a.s.}(1-U_{1})\cdots(1-U_{i-1})U_{i},

for independent random variables
U_{i}:i\geqslant 1
uniformly distributed on
[0,1]
as desired. ∎

10.2  Degrees

In this section, we study the degrees of the vertices in
T_{n}
. More precisely, for
0\leqslant i\leqslant n
the
outdegree
(number of children) of
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}⃝
in
T_{n}
will be denoted by

\mathrm{deg}^{+}_{T_{n}}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}}})=\#\{i<j\leqslant n:\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}}}\sim\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$j$}}}\mbox{ in }T_{n}\}.

Figure 10.4:
Simulation of
T_{100}
where the size and color of vertices illustrate their degrees. The first
20
vertices have their labels displayed.

10.2.1   Degree of fixed vertices

By construction, for any
i\geqslant 0
fixed, we have

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{deg}^{+}_{T_{n}}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}}}):n\geqslant 0\right)=\left(\sum_{k=i+1}^{n}B_{k}:n\geqslant 0\right),

(10.2)

where the Bernoulli random variables
B_{k}\sim\mathrm{Ber}(1/k)
are independent and of parameter
1/k
for
k\geqslant 1
. Since
\sum_{k\geqslant 1}\frac{1}{k}=\infty
, the Borel–Cantelli lemma implies that the (out)degree of any vertex
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}⃝
in
T_{n}
tends to
\infty
a.s. as
n\to\infty
. Also, by the coupling of the preceding section (or using Theorem
9.1
) we deduce that for any
n\geqslant 1

\mathrm{deg}^{+}_{T_{n}}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}}})\overset{(d)}{=}\mathcal{C}_{n},

where we recall from Proposition
9.2
that
\mathcal{C}_{n}
is the law of the number of cycles in a random uniform permutation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}_{n}
(with the CRP coupling, we have
\mathrm{deg}^{+}_{T_{n}}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}}})=\#\mathrm{Cycles}(\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}^{\mathrm{cr}})
). In particular, we deduce from Proposition
9.2
that for each
i_{0}\geqslant 0
fixed we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{deg}^{+}_{T_{n}}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i_0$}}})}{\log n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}1,

(10.3)

and we will see later (Proposition
11.13
) that the convergence actually holds almost surely.

10.2.2   Empirical degree distribution

Let us now focus on the
empirical degree distribution
in
T_{n}
: We know from (
10.3
) above that the vertices with small labels typically have a logarithmic degree, but as in any tree with
n+1
vertices, the mean degree in
T_{n}
is equal to
\frac{2n}{n+1}\to 2
as
n\to\infty
. So there must be (a lot) of vertices with small degrees. More precisely, we let
\mu_{n}
be the (random) empirical distribution of the out-degrees defined by

\mu_{n}=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=0}^{n}\delta_{\mathrm{deg}_{T_{n}}^{+}({\footnotesize\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}}}})}.

It turns out that for large
n
’s the empirical degree distribution converges towards a deterministic distribution (a stronger version will be proved in Section
11.4.1
):

Proposition 10.3

(Convergence of the empirical degree distribution)
.

The empirical distribution of the out-degrees in
T_{n}
converges in probability towards the critical geometric distribution of parameter
1/2
, i.e. for each
k_{0}\geqslant 0
we have

\mu_{n}(\{k_{0}\})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}2^{-k_{0}-1}.

Exercise 10.2
.

Prove the above proposition by computing the first and second moment of
\mu_{n}(\{k_{0}\})
.

10.2.3   Maximal degree

By (
10.3
), the typical degree of vertices with fixed label is of order
\log n
. Actually, the
largest
degree is much larger and is close to what would be the maximum of
n
i.i.d. critical geometric random variables, or in other words, as if we were sampling
n
i.i.d. degrees distributed according to the limiting empirical degree distribution computed in Proposition
10.3
:

Theorem 10.4

(Devroye & Lu)
.

Let
\mathrm{MaxDegree}(T_{n})=\max\{\mathrm{deg}^{+}_{T_{n}}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}}}):1\leqslant i\leqslant n\}
be the largest vertex (out)-degree in
T_{n}
. Then we have

\frac{\mathrm{MaxDegree}(T_{n})}{\log n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{a.s.}\frac{1}{\log 2}\approx 1.44\dots

Teasing for the proof.
The convergence in probability can be approached using the first and second moment method, but the computations are really technical… A neat proof goes through a representation of the RRT in continuous time (a.k.a. Rubbins/Athreya construction) via a Yule process, see Chapter
11
. ∎

10.3  Height

We now turn to the study of
heights
in
T_{n}
, i.e. the distances of the vertices to the root
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}⃝
in
T_{n}
. More precisely, for
n\geqslant 0
, we denote by
H_{n}
the height (or generation) of the vertex
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$n$}⃝
in the random recursive tree
T_{m}
for
m\geqslant n
(the definition does not depend on
m\geqslant n
since the vertex
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$n$}⃝
, once attached, is fixed in
T_{m}
for
m\geqslant n
).

10.3.1   Typical height

Clearly, the height of the first few vertices
H_{1},H_{2},\dots
are small and are given by the first stages in the construction of
(T_{n}:n\geqslant 0)
. We shall prove below the surprising fact that
H_{n}
has the same law as
\mathrm{deg}^{+}_{T_{n}}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}}})
, which is the law
\mathcal{C}_{n}
of the number of cycles in a uniform permutation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
:

Proposition 10.5
.

For any
n\geqslant 0
we have
H_{n}=\mathcal{C}_{n}
in law.

Remark 10.1
.

The above proposition shows that for
fixed

n\geqslant 0
, we have
H_{n}=\mathcal{C}_{n}=\mathrm{deg}^{+}_{T_{n}}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}}})
in law, but the previous equality does not hold in terms of process in
n\geqslant 0
:

(H_{n}:n\geqslant 0)\quad\neq\quad\left(\mathrm{deg}^{+}_{T_{n}}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}}}):n\geqslant 0\right).

Indeed, the process in the right-hand side is non-decreasing and tends to
+\infty
a.s. (see (
10.3
)), while the first one does not: because the degree of
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}⃝
is unbounded as
n\to\infty
, there are infinitely many vertices grafted on
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}⃝
and so infinitely many values for which
H_{n}=1
.

Figure 10.5:
Plot of a simulation of the successive heights
(H_{i}:0\leqslant i\leqslant 1000)
against the
\log
function (in red).

Proof.
Since
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$n$}⃝
is grafted to a uniform node with label
<n
we have the following recursive distributional equation:
H_{0}=0
and for
n\geqslant 1

\displaystyle H_{n}\overset{(d)}{=}1+H_{U_{n-1}},

(10.4)

where in the right-hand side
U_{n-1}\in\{0,1,2,\dots,n-1\}
is independent of the RRT defining
(H_{n}:n\geqslant 0)
. This type of equality is called a
recursive distributional equation
. Actually, we saw in the proof of Theorem
9.3
that in a uniform permutation
\boldsymbol{\sigma}_{n}
, the size
V_{n}
of the cycle containing
1
is uniformly distributed over
\{1,2,\dots,n\}
and conditionally on it the remaining (relabeled) permutation is uniform over
\mathfrak{S}_{n-V_{n}}
. In particular,
\mathcal{C}_{n}
satisfies the same recursive distributional equation as in (
10.4
):

\mathcal{C}_{n}\overset{(d)}{=}1+\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{n-V_{n}},

where on the right-hand side
(\tilde{\mathcal{C}}_{i}:i\geqslant 0)
are independent variables of law
\mathcal{C}_{i}
and also independent of the uniform variable
V_{n}\in\{1,2,\dots,n\}
. With the convention
\mathcal{C}_{0}=0
, this is sufficient to show that
\mathcal{C}_{n}
and
H_{n}
have the same law since those recursive equations (
10.4
) characterize their laws. ∎

Proposition
9.2
directly implies a central limit theorem:

\frac{H_{n}-\log n}{\sqrt{\log n}}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(d)}\mathcal{N}(0,1),

and a weak law of large number
H_{n}/\log n\to 1
in probability (but not almost surely).

10.3.2   Maximal height

As in the case of vertex degrees, the maximal height

\mathrm{Height}(T_{n}):=\max\{H_{i}:0\leqslant i\leqslant n\}

of
T_{n}
is much larger than the typical height and is also asymptotically the same as if the heights of different points were independent, that is comparable to
\sup\{\mathcal{C}_{n}^{(k)}:1\leqslant k\leqslant n\}
for independent random variable
\mathcal{C}_{n}^{(i)}
of law described in Proposition
11.13
.

Theorem 10.6

(Pittel)
.

We have

\frac{\mathrm{Height}(T_{n})}{\log n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{a.s.}\mathrm{e}.

Proof.
See Exercise
10.3
below for the upper bound using the first moment method. The lower bound can in principle be approached by the second moment method but yield to a very intricate proof. We shall prove this theorem using the continuous time embedding technique in Chapter
11
. ∎

Exercise 10.3

(Upper bound using Poisson approximation)
.

For
p\in(0,1)
and
\alpha>0
denote by
\mathrm{Ber}(p)
a Bernoulli variable with expectation
p
and by
\mathfrak{P}(\alpha)
a Poisson variable with expectation
\alpha
.

1.

Show that
\mathrm{Ber}(p)\leqslant\mathfrak{P}(-\log(1-p))
for the stochastic order and that

\mathrm{d_{TV}}(\mathrm{Ber}(p),\mathfrak{P}(p))=O(p^{2}),\quad\mbox{as }p\to 0,

where
\mathrm{d_{TV}}
is the total variation distance.

2.

Deduce that
H_{n}
is stochastically dominated by
1
plus a Poisson variable with expectation
-\sum_{i=2}^{n}\log\left(1-\frac{1}{i}\right)
.

3.

Use (
13.8
) to conclude that for all
\varepsilon>0
we have
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Height}(T_{n})>(\mathrm{e}+\varepsilon)\log n)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

4.

Prove that
\mathrm{Height}(T_{n})\leqslant(\mathrm{e}+\varepsilon)n
eventually, a.s.

Bibliographical notes.

The random recursive tree and random uniform permutations over the symmetric group are both very well studied in probability theory. Standard references are Feller
[
56
]
and the Saint-Flour lectures of Pitman
[
97
]
in particular Section 3.1 or the renowned
[
59
]
. Theorem
10.4
is due to Devroye & Lu
[
43
]
and Theorem
10.6
to Pittel
[
99
]
. See
[
106
,
61
,
18
]
for more results about the random recursive tree.

Hints for Exercises.

Exercise
10.1
: Show that
Z_{n}\,=\,\frac{R_{n}(R_{n}+1)\ldots(R_{n}+k-1)}{(n+N_{0}-1)(n+N_{0})\ldots(n+N_{0}+k-2)}\,
is a martingale for the canonical filtration and deduce the moments of the limiting proportion of red balls. See Exercise
11.4
for a calculus-free approach.

Exercise
10.3
: For the last question, use the polynomial decay of
\mathbb{P}(\mathrm{Height}(n)\geqslant(\mathrm{e}+\varepsilon)n)
obtained in
2)
along a subsequence
n=c^{k}
for
c>1
. Conclude using the fact that
n\mapsto\mathrm{Height}(T_{n})
is increasing.

XI
:
Continuous-time branching processes

Randomize to make it simpler!

In this chapter, we theorize the Poissonization technique which amounts to transforming a discrete-time process into a continuous-time version which possesses more independence properties. This will be particularly useful for urn processes and random tree growth mechanisms.

11.1  Continuous-time branching trees

Let us first recall the memorylessness property of exponential variables, which will be the crux of the continuous-time embedding technique.

11.1.1   Properties of exponential laws

In the following, for
\alpha>0
we denote by
\mathcal{E}(\alpha)
the exponential distribution of expectation
1/\alpha
, i.e. given by

\mathcal{E}(\alpha)=\alpha\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha x}\mathbf{1}_{x>0}\mathrm{d}x,

we shall say that
\alpha
is the
rate
of the exponential, since by the
memorylessness
property of the exponential distribution if
X\sim\mathcal{E}(\alpha)
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(X\in[x,x+\mathrm{d}x]\mid X\geqslant x)=\alpha\mathrm{d}x,

(11.1)

or equivalently that conditionally on
\{X\geqslant t\}
the variable
X-t
has distribution
\mathcal{E}(\alpha)
. Recall also that the memorylessness property is characteristic of the exponential and geometric laws:

Exercise 11.1

(Memorylessness)
.

Let
X
be a random variable with values in
\mathbb{R}_{+}
so that for every
a,b\in\mathrm{Supp}(\mathcal{L}(X))
we have

\mathbb{P}(X>a+b\mid X>b)=\mathbb{P}(X>a).

Show that
X
is either an exponential or a multiple of a geometric random variable.

Choosing using clocks.

Consider
X_{1}\sim\mathcal{E}(\alpha_{1}),\dots,X_{k}\sim\mathcal{E}(\alpha_{k})
a family of
k
independent exponential variables of parameters
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}
and denote by
\mathscr{M}=\min\{X_{i}:1\leqslant i\leqslant k\}
and by
\mathscr{J}\in\{1,\dots,k\}
the index at which this minimum is attained. Then we have:

Proposition 11.1

(Choosing with clocks)
.

The index
\mathscr{J}
is almost surely well-defined (there is no tie) and we have

\mathscr{M}\sim\mathcal{E}(\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{k}),\quad\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{J}=i)=\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{k}},

and conditionally on
\{\mathscr{J},\mathscr{M}\}
, the remaining variables
X_{1}-\mathscr{M},\dots,\widehat{X_{\mathscr{J}}-\mathscr{M}},\dots,X_{k}-\mathscr{M}
are independent and of laws
\mathcal{E}(\alpha_{1}),\dots,\widehat{\mathcal{E}(\alpha_{\mathscr{J}})},\dots,\mathcal{E}(\alpha_{k})
.

Proof.
This can can heuristically be explained as follows: by the memorylessness property of the exponential laws (
11.1
), the variable
\mathscr{M}
must follow an exponential law with rate
\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{k}
and given that
\mathscr{M}\in[x,x+\mathrm{d}x]
, the probability that
\mathcal{E}(\alpha_{i})
is the smallest is just proportional to the rate i.e.

\mathbb{P}(\mathscr{J}=i)=\frac{\alpha_{i}}{\alpha_{1}+\dots+\alpha_{k}}.

The remaining statement follows by the memorylessness property. More formally, since the variables are independent and have a density with respect to the Lebesgue measure, there are a.s. pairwise distincts and so
\mathscr{J}
is well-defined. Furthermore, for any positive function
\phi:\mathbb{R}_{+}\times(\mathbb{R}_{+})^{k-1}
we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\phi\Big(\mathscr{M};X_{1}-\mathscr{M},\dots,\widehat{X_{j})-\mathscr{M}},\dots,X_{k}-\mathscr{M}\Big)\mathbf{1}_{\mathscr{J}=j}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}s_{j}\alpha_{j}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha_{j}s_{j}}\int_{s_{j}}^{\infty}\left(\prod_{i\neq j}\mathrm{d}s_{i}\alpha_{i}\mathrm{e}^{-\alpha_{i}s_{i}}\right)\phi(s_{j};(s_{1}-s_{j}),\dots,\widehat{(s_{j}-s_{j})},\dots,(s_{k}-s_{j}))

\displaystyle\underset{\begin{subarray}{c}m=s_{j}\\
\tilde{s}_{i}=s_{i}-s_{j}\end{subarray}}{=}

\displaystyle\frac{\alpha_{j}}{\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}m\ (\sum_{i}\alpha_{i})\mathrm{e}^{-m(\sum_{i}\alpha_{i})}\prod_{i\neq j}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}\tilde{s}_{i}\ \ \phi(m;\tilde{s}_{1},\dots,\widehat{\tilde{s}_{j}},\dots,\tilde{s}_{k}),

and this proves the claim.
∎

A consequence of the above proposition is that if we want to sample from
\{1,2,\dots,k\}
proportionally to some weights
\alpha_{1},\dots,\alpha_{k}
; one way, which may seem strange at first glance, is to sample independent exponential clocks
(X_{i}\sim\mathcal{E}(\alpha_{i}):1\leqslant i\leqslant k)
and consider the index of the first clock that rings. The advantage of this point of view is that by Proposition
11.1
, the exponential clocks that have not rung can be further used (after subtracting the minimum) to sample according to
(\alpha_{i})
the remaining items as well!

We shall use many times the well-know extremal statistics of exponential distribution:

Lemma 11.2

(Gumbel distribution)
.

Let
X_{1},\dots,X_{n}
be i.i.d. variables of law
\mathcal{E}(1)
