is positive martingale which converges almost surely and recover the first part of the previous proposition.

Figure 13.4:
Degree’s race in the evolution of Figure
13.1
.
Curves of the renormalized vertex degrees in the scale
\sqrt{n}
(in
y
axis) in a logarithmic scale for
n
(in the
x
axis). The renormalized degree of
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}⃝
is in red, that of
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$1$}⃝
in orange and that of
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$2$}⃝
(this vertex asymptotically has the largest degree) in dark yellow.

13.2.2   Maximal degree

As for the case of the random recursive tree, one can wonder about the maximal degree in the Barabási–Albert tree process. In the RRT, the largest degree after
n
steps turned out not to be among the first nodes of the network but among the nodes arrived at time
\approx n^{0,27\dots}
, see Remark
12.1
. Here, the fast decay of the degrees enables us to show that the largest degree actually belongs to the first few nodes of the network. More precisely we have:

Theorem 13.4

(Mori)
.

With the notation of Proposition
13.3
, the random vector
(\mathcal{X}_{i}:i\geqslant 0)
almost surely satisfies
\mathcal{X}_{i}\to 0
as
i\to\infty
and the pointwise a.s. convergence can be reinforced into an almost sure convergence for the
\ell^{\infty}
metric:

\left(\frac{\mathrm{deg}_{\mathsf{T}_{n}}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}}})}{n^{1/2}}:i\geqslant 1\right)\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{a.s.\mathrm{\ for\ }\ell^{\infty}}(\mathcal{X}_{i}:i\geqslant 0).

Combining the previous result with the fact (proved in Proposition
13.3
) that the
\mathcal{X}_{i}
are positive and almost surely distinct, we deduce that the relative position
\mathrm{Pos}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}}},n)
of the degree of node
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}⃝
among
\{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}}},\dots,\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$n$}}}\}
converges almost surely as
n\to\infty
towards
\mathfrak{S}_{i}
where
\mathfrak{S}:\{0,1,2,\dots\}\to\{1,2,3,\dots\}
is a bijection. This implies in particular the convergence of the index of the largest vertex’s degree in
\mathsf{T}_{n}
.

The main technical input for the proof of Theorem
13.4
is a maximal inequality based on Proposition
11.5
:

Lemma 13.5
.

Let
(\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}_{t}:t\geqslant 0)
be the counting process of a standard Yule tree of order
2
, rate
1
, and starting from
1
particle. For all
x\geqslant 2
we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\geqslant 0}\mathrm{e}^{-t}\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}_{t}\geqslant x\right)\leqslant 2\mathrm{exp}(-x/2).

Proof.
Fix
x\geqslant 2
and denote by
\theta=\inf\{t\geqslant 0:\mathrm{e}^{-t}\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}_{t}\geqslant x\}
. On the event where the stopping time
\theta
is finite, the strong Markov property entails that conditionally on
[\mathbb{T}]_{\theta}
, the
\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}_{\theta}=\#\partial[\mathbb{T}]_{\theta}=:N
particles alive at time
\theta
will have independent offsprings distributed according to a standard Yule tree of order
2
. Recalling from Proposition
11.5
that
\mathrm{e}^{-t}\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}_{t}\to\mathcal{E}
a.s., on the event
\{\theta<\infty\}
we can write

\mathcal{E}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t}\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{-\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-t}\mathcal{Y}^{(2),i}_{t}=\mathrm{e}^{-\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{E}_{i},

where on the right-hand side, the variables
(\mathcal{E}_{i}:i\geqslant 1)
are i.i.d. exponential variables of rate
1
independent of
N
. Using the easy fact that
\inf_{k\geqslant 1}\mathbb{P}(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{E}_{i}\geqslant k/2)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-x/2}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}\geqslant x/2)

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\theta<\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\mathrm{e}^{-\theta}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{E}_{i}\geqslant\frac{x}{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\underset{N\mathrm{e}^{-\theta}\geqslant x}{\geqslant}

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbf{1}_{\theta<\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{E}_{i}\geqslant\frac{N}{2}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\geqslant

\displaystyle\inf_{k\geqslant 1}\mathbb{P}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\mathcal{E}_{i}\geqslant k/2\right)\cdot\mathbb{P}(\theta<\infty)\geqslant\frac{1}{2}\mathbb{P}(\theta<\infty).

∎

Proof of Theorem
13.4
.
Given the work done in the proof of Proposition
13.3
, the convergence for the
\ell^{\infty}
metric follows if we can show that

\lim_{m\to\infty}\sup_{i\geqslant m}\sup_{n\geqslant 1}\frac{\mathrm{deg}_{\mathsf{T}_{n}}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}}})}{\sqrt{n}}=0,\quad a.s.

or via the continuous time representation that

\displaystyle\lim_{T\to\infty}\sup_{{\footnotesize\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}}}}\mathrm{\ created\ after\ }T}\sup_{t\geqslant T}\frac{\mathrm{deg}_{\{\hskip-2.04861pt\{\mathbb{F}\}\hskip-2.04861pt\}_{t}}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}}})}{\mathrm{e}^{t}}=0,\quad a.s.

(13.1)

For
t>A
, a new splitting appears in
\mathbb{F}
with intensity
\#\partial[\mathbb{F}]_{t}\ \mathrm{d}t
, this creates a new vertex in
\{\hskip-2.5pt\{\mathbb{F}\}\hskip-2.5pt\}_{t}
and the probability that such a vertex gets a degree larger than
\varepsilon\mathrm{e}^{u}
at some later time
u=s+t\geqslant t
is upper bounded by

\mathbb{P}(\sup_{s\geqslant 0}\mathrm{e}^{-s}\mathcal{Y}^{(2)}_{s}\geqslant\varepsilon\mathrm{e}^{t})\underset{\mathrm{Lem.\ }\ref{lem:techdeg}}{\leqslant}2\exp(-\varepsilon\mathrm{e}^{t}/2).

We deduce that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{{\footnotesize\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}}}}\mathrm{\ created\ after\ }T}\mathbf{1}\left\{\sup_{t\geqslant A}\frac{\mathrm{deg}_{\{\hskip-2.04861pt\{\mathbb{F}\}\hskip-2.04861pt\}_{t}}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}}})}{\mathrm{e}^{t}}\geqslant\varepsilon\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leqslant

\displaystyle\int_{A}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\ \mathbb{E}[\#\partial[\mathbb{F}]_{t}]\cdot 2\exp(-\varepsilon\mathrm{e}^{t}/2)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{A}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}t\ 2\mathrm{e}^{2t}\cdot 2\exp(-\varepsilon\mathrm{e}^{t}/2).

For
\varepsilon>0
fixed, the above integral can be made arbitrarily small provided that
A>0
is chosen large enough. This implies (
13.1
). ∎

13.2.3   Empirical degree distribution

As in Section
10.2.2
we can also study the
empirical degree distribution
in
\mathsf{T}_{n}
: We let
\nu_{n}
be the (random) empirical distribution of the out-degrees defined by

\nu_{n}=\frac{1}{n+1}\sum_{i=0}^{n}\delta_{\mathrm{deg}_{\mathsf{T}_{n}}^{+}({\footnotesize\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}}}})}.

As for Proposition
10.3
, the empirical degree distribution converges towards a deterministic distribution which now has an interesting polynomial tail behavior:

Theorem 13.6

(Convergence of the empirical degree distribution)
.

The empirical distribution of the out-degrees in
\mathsf{T}_{n}
converges in probability towards an explicit deterministic law: for each
k\geqslant 0
we have

\nu_{n}(\{k\})\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}\frac{4}{(k+1)(k+2)(k+3)}.

Proof.
We obviously use the construction of
\mathsf{T}_{n}=\{\hskip-2.5pt\{\mathbb{F}\}\hskip-2.5pt\}_{\tau_{n}}
valid for all
n\geqslant 1
simultaneously. The same proof as for Proposition
11.14
shows that

\frac{\mathsf{D}_{k}([\mathbb{F}]_{t})}{\#\partial[\mathbb{F}]_{t}}\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathrm{e}^{-2t}\mathbb{E}[\mathsf{D}_{k}([\mathbb{F}]_{t})],

where

\mathsf{D}_{k}([\mathbb{F}]_{t}):=\#\Big\{u\in\{\hskip-2.5pt\{\mathbb{F}\}\hskip-2.5pt\}_{t}\backslash\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}}}:\mathrm{deg}^{+}_{\{\hskip-2.04861pt\{\mathbb{F}\}\hskip-2.04861pt\}_{t}}(u)=k\Big\},

and where the limit exists. We compute the expectation of
{\mathsf{D}}_{k}([\mathbb{T}]_{t})
, the number of vertices different from
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}⃝
and of degree
k\geqslant 1
in
\{\hskip-2.5pt\{\mathbb{T}\}\hskip-2.5pt\}_{t}
, in a single contracted Yule tree of order
3
. As in Section
11.4.3
, recall that a new vertex is created at time
s
with intensity
\#\partial[\mathbb{T}]_{s}
and by (
11.3
), this vertex has degree
k
at time
t
with probability
\mathrm{e}^{-(t-s)}(1-\mathrm{e}^{-(t-s)})^{k-1}
. Recalling that
\mathbb{E}[\#\partial[\mathbb{T}]_{t}]=\mathrm{e}^{2t}
we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-2t}\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[{\mathsf{D}}_{k}([\mathbb{T}]_{t})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{e}^{-2t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}s\,\mathbb{E}[\#\partial[\mathbb{T}]_{s}]\cdot\mathrm{e}^{-(t-s)}(1-\mathrm{e}^{-(t-s)})^{k-1}

\displaystyle\underset{u=t-s}{=}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{e}^{-3u}(1-\mathrm{e}^{-u})^{k-1}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathrm{d}u\,\mathrm{e}^{-3u}(1-\mathrm{e}^{-u})^{k-1}

\displaystyle\underset{x=\mathrm{e}^{-u}}{=}

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{d}x\,x^{2}(1-x)^{k-1}=\frac{2}{k(k+1)(k+2)}

In the case of two trees, we also have for any
k\geqslant 1

\frac{{\mathrm{D}}_{k}([\mathbb{F}]_{t})}{\#\partial[\mathbb{F}]_{t}}\xrightarrow[t\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}\frac{2}{k(k+1)(k+2)},

which proves the result since the number of vertices in
\{\hskip-2.5pt\{\mathbb{F}\}\hskip-2.5pt\}_{t}
is half of
\#\partial[\mathbb{F}]_{t}
. ∎

Remark 13.1

(Scale-free property)
.

The fact that the empirical degree distribution
\nu_{n}
converges towards a limiting law
\nu_{\infty}
with a polynomial tail behavior
\nu_{\infty}(\{k\})\approx k^{-\alpha}
with
\alpha>0
is usually refer to as the
scale-free
property. In the case of the Barabi–Albert trees the tail with exponent
\alpha=3
is coherent with the fact that the largest degree in
\mathsf{T}_{n}
is of order
\sqrt{n}=n^{1/(\alpha-1)}
which is the order of magnitude of the maximum of
n
i.i.d. samplings according to
\nu_{\infty}
.

13.3  Height

We finish by studying the maximal height in
\mathsf{T}_{n}
. The preferential attachment mechanism do yield to smaller trees compared to the uniform attachment case, but they stay of logarithmic order:

Theorem 13.7

(Pittel)
.

We have

\frac{\mathrm{Height}(\mathsf{T}_{n})}{\log n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{a.s.}c\approx 1.79\dots,

where
c=(2\gamma)^{-1}
for
\gamma
the solution to
\gamma\mathrm{e}^{1+\gamma}=1
.

Sketch of proof.
The proof follows the same strategy as that of Theorem
10.6
presented in Section
10.6
. Similar to (
12.2.1
), a particle
u\in\partial[\mathbb{F}]_{t}
is associated with a vertex in
\{\hskip-2.5pt\{\mathbb{F}\}\hskip-2.5pt\}_{t}
whose distance to the root
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}⃝
in
\{\hskip-2.5pt\{\mathbb{F}\}\hskip-2.5pt\}_{t}
is equal to the number of branch points along the spine for which the lineage to
u
is the right-most. When
u=\bullet
is the distinguished particle of
[\mathbb{T}^{\bullet}]_{t}
under
\mathbb{P}_{\delta_{2}}
, branchings happens at rate
3
and a third of them is of the above form. By the many to one formula (Theorem
12.2
) we then have

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{u\in\partial[\mathbb{T}]_{t}}\mathbf{1}\{\mathrm{dist}_{\{\hskip-2.04861pt\{\mathbb{T}\}\hskip-2.04861pt\}_{t}}(u,\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}}})\geqslant\alpha t\}\right]=\mathrm{e}^{2t}\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{P}(t)\geqslant\alpha t).

By Lemma
13.8
, when
\alpha=\gamma^{1}+\varepsilon
the previous display converges to
0
exponentially fast with
t\to\infty
(notice that
a=\gamma^{-1}
is solution to
a\log a-(a-1)=2
). Since there are roughly
n\approx\mathrm{e}^{2t}
vertices at time
t
in
\{\hskip-2.5pt\{\mathbb{F}\}\hskip-2.5pt\}_{t}
we deduce using the same arguments as in Section
12.2.1
that the height of
\mathsf{T}_{n}
is eventually less than
\frac{\gamma^{-1}}{2}n
as
n\to\infty
a.s. The lower bound follows mutatis mutandis the same lines as in Section
12.2.1
and we leave it as an exercise for the reader. ∎

Bibliographical notes.

Although generally attributed to Albert & Barabási
[
17
]
which is one of the most cited papers in mathematics with more than 45 000 citations up to 2023, the model of linear preferential attachment tree has been studied before (at least) by Szymanski
[
109
]
and Mahmoud
[
89
]
. This is a very good example of Stigler’s law of eponymy. Exercise
13.1
was suggested by Pavel Krapivsky. The almost sure convergence of the largest degrees (Theorem
13.4
) is due to Mori
[
91
]
. Theorem
13.7
is first proved in
[
99
]
using the continuous time embedding technique.

Hints for Exercises.

Exercise
13.1
: A new vertex attaches to
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}⃝
with probability proportional
\mathrm{deg}^{+}_{\mathfrak{T}_{n}}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}}})+1
which is equal to
\mathrm{deg}_{\mathfrak{T}_{n}}(\raisebox{0.5pt}{\textcircled{\raisebox{-.9pt} {$i$}}})
except for the root
\raisebox{-.9pt} {$0$}⃝
which has a small bias.

Appendix

So the last shall be first.

(Matthew 20:16)

Large deviations for Poisson random variables

Let us state a simple lemma on Poisson random variables which we used many times in these lecture notes. Recall that
\mathfrak{P}(a)
is a Poisson variable of mean
a>0
.

Lemma 13.8

(Large deviations and maximum of i.i.d. Poisson random variables)
.

For
a>0
denote by
I(a):=a\log a-(a-1)
then for all
t\geqslant 0
we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{P}(t)>at)\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-tI(a)}\quad\mbox{ if }a>1\quad\mbox{ and }\quad\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{P}(t)<at)\leqslant\mathrm{e}^{-tI(a)}\quad\mbox{ if }0<a<1.

Fix
c\geqslant 0
and let
X_{1},\dots,X_{\lfloor n^{c}\rfloor}
be i.i.d. random variables with Poisson law of expectation
\log n
. Then we have

\frac{\log{\max_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n^{c}}X_{i}}}{\log n}\xrightarrow[n\to\infty]{(\mathbb{P})}x_{c},\quad\mbox{ with $x_{c}\geqslant 1$ solution to }I(x_{c})=c,

and furthermore
\mathbb{P}(\max_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n^{c}}X_{i}\leqslant n^{x_{c}-\varepsilon})
tends to
0
stretched-exponentially fast.

Proof.
Suppose
a>1
and let us apply a standard exponential Markov’s inequality to write for
\lambda>0

\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{P}(t)>at)\leqslant\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{e}^{\lambda\mathfrak{P}(t)}]}{\mathrm{e}^{\lambda at}}=\mathrm{exp}(t((\mathrm{e}^{\lambda}-1)-\lambda a))\underset{\lambda=\log a}{\leqslant}\exp(-tI(a)).

The case
a<1
is dealt with similarly using negative
\lambda
. For the second point notice that for
x\geqslant 1
we have using the first point

\mathbb{P}(\max_{1\leqslant i\leqslant n^{c}}X_{i}\leqslant n^{x})=(1-\mathbb{P}(\mathfrak{P}(\log n)>x\log n))^{n^{c}}=\exp\left(-n^{c-I(x)+o(1)}\right),

and so the above probability tends to
0
stretched-exponentially fast if
I(x)<c
and to
1
if
I(x)>c
. ∎

First part of Lemma
13.8
is known under the name of “BennettÕs inequality” (see Terence Tao’s blog for a nice sharpening of it).
